- **[ARISE: Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement for Continual Multimodal Forgery Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36509)**  `arXiv:2610.36509`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Yaxiong Wang, Yujiao Wu, Lianwei Wu, Qi Zhang, Li Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal forgery detection is commonly studied under a static assumption: training attacks are expected to approximate those encountered after deployment. This assumption becomes increasingly fragile as generators, editing systems, and multimodal large language models continually introduce new forgery mechanisms and forensic evidence. We formulate this problem as attack-incremental continual multimodal forgery detection, where the binary real/fake interface remains fixed while emerging attacks must be learned without forgetting previous ones. We introduce Fable-Bench, which organizes attack evolution through complementary joint-modality, text-side, and image-side protocols. We further propose ARISE (Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement), motivated by the open-ended forged class and the comparatively reusable geometry of authentic content. ARISE fits a real-only Gaussian mixture model to projected authentic replay representations as a compact historical reference. Meanwhile, the proposed Gaussian-Responsibility-Aware Stability Preservation (GRASP) derives a sample-adaptive anisotropic metric that strongly constrains historically stable representation directions while retaining plasticity elsewhere. Our Vulnerability-Aware Replay (VAR) further estimates stage-wise vulnerability from authentic-density degradation, forged-to-authentic boundary confusion, and authentic-mode occupancy drift, then assigns bounded replay emphasis to fragile stages. All adaptive signals are obtained from training-time replay statistics, while inference uses only the original multimodal backbone and binary classification head. Experiments across three attack-evolution protocols and multiple detector architectures demonstrate improved historical retention and final average performance over representative continual-learning baselines.
  </details>

- **[Spatial-Frequency Learning for Scanning Transmission Electron Microscopy Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36487)**  `arXiv:2610.36487`  
  _Hesong Li, Ying Fu, Ziqi Wu, Ruiwen Shao, Tao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scanning Transmission Electron Microscopy (STEM) enables the observation of atomic arrangements at sub-angstrom resolution and plays a key role in the discovery and characterization of advanced materials. Raw STEM images are often noisy and blurred due to multiple degradations. Although recent deep learning methods improve STEM images, their performance remains limited under severe degradations. In this work, we propose a spatial-frequency learning framework for STEM image enhancement. Specifically, we design a spatial-frequency fusion network that exploits periodic atomic arrangements in the frequency domain and restores local atomic details in the spatial domain. Spatial- and frequency-domain features are enhanced separately and then fused. We also present a STEM noise calibration method for synthesizing more realistic STEM images. The parameters of scan noise, pointwise noise, and background noise are estimated through statistical analysis and fitting of real STEM images. Using these calibrated parameters, we construct a diverse dataset containing a wider range of atomic arrangements, imaging modes, and degradations. Random masks combine different atomic arrangements to simulate interfaces between multiple materials. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art enhancement performance on synthetic datasets and real images of advanced materials while improving atomic localization accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion-Uni: Elastic Self-Refining Unified Diffusion LLM with Cross-Paradigm On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36466)**  `arXiv:2610.36466`  
  _Shufan Li, Hanrong Ye, Greg Heinrich, Yonggan Fu, Haotian Zhang, Sifei Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion-Uni (NLD-Uni), a unified discrete diffusion large language model (DLLM) for visual understanding and generation tasks. Compared with prior unified diffusion models, NLD-Uni introduces two major innovations. First, we propose a unified cross-paradigm on-policy distillation (OPD) framework that successfully transfers the visual understanding ability of autoregressive VLMs and visual generation ability of continuous flow models, to a unified DLLM. Second, NLD-Uni incorporates a novel progressive self-refining diffusion process that gradually corrupts each token across multiple steps during training, as opposed to adopting the conventional masked diffusion formulation that directly corrupts a clean image token to an uninformative mask token. At inference, this design allows NLD-Uni to make intermediate commit to a token, enabling progressive self-refinement for better visual quality. Through extensive experiments, we show that NLD-Uni achieves state-of-the-art performance among unified DLLMs on diverse tasks including visual question-answering, multi-modal reasoning, object grounding, text-to-image generation, and image editing.
  </details>

- **[ClusterSplat: Semantic Cluster Selection for 3D Visual Grounding in Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36462)**  `arXiv:2610.36462`  
  _Jaesung Lee, Hyeontaek Hwang, Gabriel Manalu, Daeyoung Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study 3D visual grounding in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), where a referring expression should identify a target object as both a rendered 2D mask and a set of explicit 3D Gaussians. Prior methods typically extract 3D targets by applying heuristic thresholding or top-ratio filtering to dense primitive-level relevance scores. However, these scores often vary substantially across scenes and prompts, making such heuristics sensitive and leading to unstable targeting. ClusterSplat addresses this by reframing 3D target selection as cluster-level selection over scene-adaptive candidates. The method first learns instance-aware Gaussian features, forms feature-aware seed voxels, and merges neighboring seed voxels when a description-length criterion decreases, producing a scene-specific set of cluster candidates. Given a referring expression, a query-conditioned cluster scorer ranks the cluster candidates with a lightweight MLP, and the selected cluster directly defines the explicit 3D target while its cluster score is rendered for the 2D mask. On ScanRefer, ClusterSplat achieves the best rendered 2D segmentation and explicit 3D target metrics among the compared 3DGS baselines. It also obtains the best average Ref-LERF scores, supporting the same trend under fine-grained referring descriptions. On ScanNet v2 class name queries, ClusterSplat achieves the strongest rendered 2D results and the highest 3D matching accuracy, while maintaining competitive 3D IoU. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Full Collapse Is Suboptimal: Controlled Partial Neural Collapse for Long-Tailed Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36460)**  `arXiv:2610.36460`  
  _Hyeontaek Hwang, Cheonghwan Hur, Daeyoung Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent long-tailed recognition (LTR) methods incorporate neural collapse (NC) and align features to a fixed Equiangular Tight Frame (ETF) as a uniform target for all classes. However, forcing tail classes to fully collapse over-contracts their samples and weakens generalization. To address this, we propose a controlled partial neural collapse, realized through two coordinated controls on a backbone: the Class-Adaptive Sparse Activation (CASA) classifier and the ETF-constrained adaptive margin (ETF-AM) loss. Rather than forcing a uniform full collapse, this regime allows head classes to collapse along the ETF while tail classes retain within-class diversity. This prevents the tail from being forced into full collapse, which is suboptimal under imbalance. To set the dimensionality of collapse, CASA induces class-related node specialization through feature-conditioned gating so that tail classes occupy their own feature directions. For the clearance axis, ETF-AM applies a frequency-adaptive margin, with smaller margins for head classes and larger for tail classes. On CIFAR100-LT, ImageNet-LT, Places-LT, and iNaturalist-2018, our method achieves the best overall accuracy over prior methods. In the empirical analysis, within-class spread increases monotonically from head to tail classes, consistent with tail classes retaining variability rather than fully collapsing.
  </details>

- **[FORESEE-MS: Toward Prospective Spatial Forecasting of Future Multiple Sclerosis Lesions from Baseline FLAIR MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36428)**  `arXiv:2610.36428`  
  _Maryam Akhavan Aghdam, Sudip Mittal, Noorbakhsh Amiri Golilarz, Shahram Rahimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional multiple sclerosis (MS) image analysis has primarily focused on retrospectively detecting and segmenting lesions. This study investigates whether baseline FLAIR MRI can identify regions at risk for future lesion formation beyond population-level spatial patterns. We introduce FORESEE-MS, an evaluation protocol that uses a fold-specific population-prior atlas as a baseline and assesses predictions using pooled voxel-level discrimination (AUROC and AUPRC) and within-patient lesion localization (Recall@Top-K%), with patient-level bootstrap confidence intervals. We evaluated five deep learning models (3D U-Net, Attn+ResBlock+VAE, ViT, and two SwinUNETR variants) using 5-fold cross-validation on 39 subjects from the MSSEG-2 dataset. The atlas achieved the highest pooled voxel-level AUROC and AUPRC, with no deep learning model reliably outperforming it on either metric. However, the 3D U-Net and Attn+ResBlock+VAE achieved higher mean voxel-level within-patient Recall@Top-K% than the atlas at all evaluated thresholds, although most paired confidence intervals for the differences included zero. These findings suggest that baseline FLAIR may contain patient-specific predictive information, but in this small cohort, it remains uncertain whether this information improves lesion forecasting beyond a population-prior atlas.
  </details>

- **[SO-101 Bench: Accessible Spatial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36420)**  `arXiv:2610.36420`  
  _Truman Hickok_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-cost arms such as the SO-101 have become the most common entry point to robot learning, yet no released policy performs spatially constrained, language-conditioned tasks on them out of the box, and existing SO-101 benchmarks run only on physical hardware with simple tasks and few held-out objects. We introduce SO-101 Bench, a simulated benchmark for language-conditioned spatial manipulation built on an Isaac Lab digital twin of a real SO-101 setup. It has four tasks scored as five families, 138 objects in seen, unseen-instance and unseen-class splits, 916 evaluation episodes with subsets for held-out appearances, rotate-to-fit planning and occlusion memory, and 3,629 simulated demonstrations on which every learned policy is fine-tuned under an equal compute budget. The twin ranks policies as the real robot does: five policies trained without real data, four VLAs and an LLM agent, keep their exact ranking when the same weights run on the physical arm in an unseen environment with unseen objects (Spearman rho = 1.00, MMRV = 0.000), although simulation overestimates success. Across nine VLAs and world-action models, the best learned policies score near 61%, with spatial placement, unseen object classes and rotate-to-fit planning remaining open. An LLM agent that writes code and hands grasping to a learned policy scores 83.2%, and an ablation shows that this lead comes from the choice of harness and the choice of language model.
  </details>

- **[SpeeDiff: Scalable Pixel-Anchored End-to-End Latent Diffusion Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36419)**  `arXiv:2610.36419`  
  _Bingliang Zhang, Wenda Chu, Yizhuo Li, Linjie Yang, Yisong Yue, Katherine L. Bouman, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Scalable Pixel-anchored End-to-end Diffusion (SpeeDiff), a latent diffusion method that jointly trains the VAE and the diffusion model from scratch. In principle, joint training allows the diffusion loss gradient to directly guide the VAE encoder, encouraging the formation of a generation-friendly latent space and potentially yielding faster convergence than the conventional two-stage approach with a pretrained frozen VAE. However, a naive end-to-end implementation severely degrades performance, as unrestricted backpropagation of the diffusion loss leads to latent space collapse. Our main technical contribution is a simple yet effective Tweedie Pixel Reconstruction (TPR) loss, which provides additional pixel-level feedback by decoding a predicted clean latent from an intermediate noisy state using Tweedie's formula, thereby alleviating collapse. Furthermore, our method enables jointly scaling a fully transformer-based architecture and enhances representation alignment within the end-to-end framework. Our SpeeDiff-XL model achieves over 140x and 61x faster training compared to Vanilla SiT and REPA, respectively, while attaining an FID of 1.50 without guidance on ImageNet 256x256 generation. With a more efficient 32x compressed VAE, our model further reaches an FID of 1.53 without guidance on ImageNet 512x512 generation.
  </details>

- **[BoxWorld: Let World Engines Control and Video Generators Render](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36371)**  `arXiv:2610.36371`  
  _Zike Wu, Ahmed Hatem, Chintan Trivedi, Wallace Michel Pinto Lira, Xuebin Qin, Lele Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We tackle the challenge of building real-time interactive video worlds with user-defined behaviors. Such worlds must track object states, apply interaction rules, and render the resulting consequences as users act. Yet existing end-to-end video world models typically learn interaction dynamics within the generator, making it difficult to specify object-level rules and reason about their consequences. In this work, we introduce BoxWorld, which separates interaction reasoning from generative rendering. Given user-specified rules, a coding agent creates a Box World Engine that determines interaction outcomes and exposes the resulting object states through a lightweight interface of layout boxes, persistent object attributes, and dynamic status variables, leaving appearance and physical effects to generative rendering. A Box-State Generative Renderer then renders this evolving state stream into video autoregressively, enabling online control over object motion, state switches, and conditional events. Rendering this state stream is non-trivial: the renderer must respond to object-level state updates in real time while remaining coherent with the generated history. To this end, we introduce a three-stage training strategy that learns object grounding from open-domain videos and state transitions from gameplay videos, both annotated automatically, and finally performs rollout distillation on self-generated histories. We further propose PairedStateBench, a paired benchmark for fine-grained interaction control. BoxWorld substantially outperforms end-to-end world models and remains competitive across multiple public benchmarks. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CrowdCC: Jointly Benchmarking Corruption Robustness and Deployment Quantization in Crowd Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36340)**  `arXiv:2610.36340`  
  _S Mahmudul Hasan, Lu Peng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crowd-counting models deployed on edge devices face two shifts: inputs are degraded along the capture and transmission pipeline, while models are quantized to satisfy resource constraints. Yet corruption robustness and deployment quantization are typically evaluated separately. We introduce CrowdCC, a controlled corruption benchmark that jointly evaluates these effects using 23 deployment-motivated corruptions at three severity levels, four crowd-counting datasets, and 26 models using A100 TensorRT engines at FP32, FP16, and post-training INT8 precision, paired with throughput and energy measurements from corresponding Jetson Orin NX configurations. The corruptions span six families, with head annotations adjusted when geometry or visibility changes. Our evaluation shows that capture-chain corruptions consistently produce the largest degradation across the evaluated models, while crowd density affects absolute and relative error differently. Clean accuracy does not fully predict corrupted performance. INT8 alters corruption robustness substantially more than FP16, with no systematic direction, and does not consistently improve latency or energy. INT8 inference efficiency is strongly associated with reformat overhead in the compiled engine, while slowdowns are also observed under limited INT8 coverage. Precision changes corrupted-error rankings and robustness-cost frontiers, showing why model selection must consider corruption robustness and measured deployment cost together. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CRLoMTL: Conflict-Resolution through Low-rank matrices for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36337)**  `arXiv:2610.36337`  
  _Ahmed Agiza, Tasneem Shaffee, Rahil Badkul, Sherief Reda_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task learning (MTL) improves model efficiency by sharing representations across related vision tasks, but it often suffers from negative transfer when task updates interfere with one another. Existing conflict-mitigation methods typically operate in gradient space, requiring explicit task-gradient manipulation and additional optimization steps. We propose Conflict-Resolution through Low-Rank Matrices for Multi-Task Learning (CRLoMTL), a parameter-efficient framework that mitigates task interference in the low-rank parameter-update space. CRLoMTL learns task-specific LoRA updates through task-specific passes, where each task updates its own low-rank matrices, and integrates these updates through global shared-subspace fusion. Specifically, we introduce a shared-subspace resolver based on singular value decomposition (SVD), referred to as the SVD resolver, which extracts a common low-dimensional orthonormal basis across task adapters and projects task updates into this shared space before merging them into a single deployment-time update. The resulting update can be folded into the backbone, preserving the inference structure of the original model without additional runtime branches. Experiments with Swin Transformer backbones on PASCAL-Context, NYUD-v2, and CelebA show that CRLoMTL achieves a favorable accuracy-parameter trade-off, improving over single-task baselines by up to 2.77% on PASCAL-Context, 5.03% on NYUD-v2, and 3.50% on CelebA, while training only a small fraction of the parameters used by single-task models.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Inference Acceleration for Video Large Language Models: Methods, Trade-offs, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36327)**  `arXiv:2610.36327`  
  _Yuqi Li, Chuanyue Yan, Neil Rodrigues, Xianglong Wang, Xiaoqin Feng, Junming Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video Large Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in video understanding and reasoning, but processing long visual sequences incurs substantial computational, latency, and memory overhead. This survey examines training-free inference acceleration, focusing on reducing inference costs while preserving task-relevant spatiotemporal evidence. We organize existing methods by their intervention stages in the inference pipeline, covering input, visual encoding, projector, prefill, and decoding. Post-training quantization (PTQ) is discussed separately as a cross-stage extension with varying calibration requirements. We analyze how intervention position affects evidence preservation and computational savings, and why local efficiency improvements may not translate into end-to-end acceleration. We complement this analysis with quantitative evidence from prior studies and our own experiments under a shared evaluation setting to assess accuracy-latency trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Correcting Bias in MLLM for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36210)**  `arXiv:2610.36210`  
  _Xusheng Liang, Jianqiao Sun, Hao Zhang, Yulei Niu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiawei Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering is challenging since the answer often hinges on a few decisive moments scattered throughout the video, while memory constraints force Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to sample frames at extremely low rates, resulting in sparsity that risks omitting key evidence. Though aggressively enlarging the context size is intuitive, we argue that performance remains fundamentally constrained by the bias, \emph{i.e.}, the misleading cues caused by spurious \textit{linguistic} correlations and salient yet irrelevant \textit{visual} observations. In this paper, we first introduce {S-MME}, a benchmark designed to systematically diagnose shortcut-biased behavior in MLLMs. To mitigate this failure, we further propose a training-free framework, Counterfactual Long-video Evidence-Aware Reasoning (CLEAR). By treating the full video-question pair as a complete view, CLEAR estimates bias effects via augmented views by only keeping the question or locally salient visual clips. Then, we intervene hidden states to mitigate the discrepancy at inference such that the correct answer can be predicted confidently. With experimental studies across five benchmarks, we show that our method is generalizable and can be integrated in different MLLMs, yielding consistent gains in prediction and robustness. We hope CLEAR can offer a complementary direction in test-time scaling for long-video understanding.
  </details>

- **[UT-HMR: Uncertainty-Guided Temporal Modeling for Robust 3D Human Mesh Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36203)**  `arXiv:2610.36203`  
  _Zhibo Wang, Sirisha Rambhatla, Yuhao Chen, Maria Koshkina, David Clausi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular 3D human mesh recovery (HMR) remains unreliable under weak observations caused by long camera-to-person distances, occlusion, and motion blur. In challenging scenarios such as sports, these factors make the problem highly ambiguous, as different 3D poses may yield similar 2D projections. This ambiguity is especially severe for one-stage HMR, which recovers all meshes directly from the full image without person crops or external 2D cues. To handle this ambiguity, we present UT-HMR ( Uncertainty-guided Temporal HMR), to our knowledge the first one-stage HMR framework to capture correlated pose uncertainty across joints. Each person query predicts a Gaussian distribution over the full SMPL pose with a compact low-rank-plus-diagonal covariance, which is converted into joint-wise precision weights for heteroscedastic supervision while keeping inference deterministic. Temporal early fusion further injects neighboring-frame evidence before pose decoding, supplying the visual evidence needed to resolve the ambiguity that the uncertainty captures. With a lightweight ViT-B backbone, UT-HMR achieves state-of-the-art results on VIP-HockeyPose-v2 (ice hockey) and WorldPose (soccer), reducing MPJPE, PA-MPJPE, and PVE by up to 4.7%, 3.8%, and 5.6%, and remains competitive on 3DPW against methods with larger backbones and higher input resolutions.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Estimate: Wiener-Informed Decoding for Easier Training of Pixel-Space Flow Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36192)**  `arXiv:2610.36192`  
  _Ziqiao Wang, Yang You_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning velocity fields directly in pixel space is difficult and slow. We show that coarse second-order image statistics can ease this task: frozen Wiener estimates improve velocity learning, with gains extending beyond the linear predictions they explicitly provide. Moreover, we observe similar but complementary gains from frozen bases and pixel decoding. These observations motivate Wiener-informed Decoding (WiD), which uses statistical knowledge both for direct prediction and to organize decoder computation without modifying native blocks. WiD combines a trainable Wiener prediction path with coefficient interfaces that inject features into the native decoder and return its predicted coefficient corrections, with these components initialized with data statistics. All introduced parameters, including the spectrum and coordinate transforms, are trained jointly under the original pixel-space velocity objective. Across model scales and decoder architectures, WiD accelerates training and improves generation quality with modest parameter overhead. It also yields smoother gradient trajectories and remains effective with representation alignment. WiD improves class-conditional and text-to-image generation across different resolutions. On ImageNet 256 x 256, PixelDiT-XL with WiD reaches FID 1.53 after 500 epochs, achieving comparable quality to PixelDiT with 37.5% fewer training epochs. Statistical denoisers contribute beyond the estimate: their computational structure can also ease pixel-space training.
  </details>

- **[PrinGS: Privacy Preservation in Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36168)**  `arXiv:2610.36168`  
  _Gaurav Behera, Abhinav Raundhal, Archisha Panda, Saumya Balina, Gargi Shroff, Ponnurangam Kumaraguru_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has rapidly become the preferred representation for high-fidelity novel view synthesis and 3D scene reconstruction. However, the learned representation encodes sensitive visual information including human faces, license plates, documents, and computer screens, raising significant privacy concerns when trained splats are shared or deployed. In this work, we address the underexplored problem of privacy preservation in Gaussian Splatting by proposing a representation-level anonymization framework that directly anonymizes a trained splat. Given a trained splat and a user-provided reference view identifying sensitive content, our method localizes the corresponding regions in the underlying 3D representation and directly anonymizes them, ensuring that the sensitive information is absent from all subsequently rendered novel views while preserving the overall scene geometry and visual fidelity. We achieve this post-training anonymization in less than 1 second. Furthermore, we extend our framework with a privacy-by-design training strategy that prevents sensitive content from being embedded into the representation during optimization, enabling the generation of privacy-preserving splats directly from training images. Our method achieves a remarkably low Face Similarity Measure (FSM) of 9.79% at a retained Face Detection Rate (FDR) of 39.47% and makes sensitive text completely unreadable with an OCR Character Error Rate of 1.00. Our work aims to serve as the primary stepping stone towards privacy-aware 3D representations and motivates further research along these directions.
  </details>

- **[Any3R: Feedforward 3D Reconstruction from Any Camera Type](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36150)**  `arXiv:2610.36150`  
  _Jingong Chen, Qingwen Zhang, Youming Deng, Chulwoo Pack, Olaf Wysocki, Boris Jutzi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry foundation models recover camera poses and dense geometry from a set of images with a feed-forward network, but they expect pinhole images. That assumption is inside the backbone too: the positional encoding and the training loss both bind an image position to a direction in space, and both depend on the lens. We condition both on the dense ray field, the unit direction through every pixel, which describes any central camera without a lens type, distortion coefficients or a bound on field of view. Angular coordinates give the rotary encoding the measured ray angle of each patch, evaluated per view, and solid-angle weighting scales the point loss by the area each pixel covers on the sphere, so neither the encoding nor the supervision depends on the lens. Any3R is therefore a feed-forward model that takes any central camera, and is robust to mixing many camera types per scene, with no retraining per lens. On a benchmark of thirteen central cameras across three datasets, where every method reconstructs with its own predicted camera poses, it is the most consistently accurate method. Any3R enables scalable 3D reconstruction from heterogeneous crowdsourced and archival imagery collections sourced across diverse camera types. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Learning and Reasoning with Geometry: How Generation Helps Spatial Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36022)**  `arXiv:2610.36022`  
  _Rui Yang, Yanwei Li, Sili Chen, Jingjia Huang, Yi Lin, Hengshuang Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image and video generators can produce spatially consistent visual content, but how to use this capability to improve spatial understanding remains underexplored. We study this problem from two perspectives: geometric supervision during training and explicit geometric evidence during inference. To this end, we introduce a unified geometric understanding and generation model (GeoUG) that integrates depth and multi-view generation with spatial understanding. First, we design a multi-scale depth codec that enables metric-depth generation using a pretrained visual autoencoder, and show that joint training yields task-dependent gains in spatial understanding. Second, we introduce 3DCueBench to control geometric difficulty while balancing selected 2D cues, and find that generated depth provides larger reasoning gains on questions requiring fine-grained geometric discrimination. Building on this finding, we develop adaptive interleaved reasoning, in which GeoUG first drafts an answer and then decides whether to generate geometric evidence to confirm or revise it. We train this decision through reinforcement learning, balancing accuracy gains over retaining the draft against generation cost. GeoUG achieves circular accuracies of 97.9%, 90.8%, and 94.2% on CV-Bench-3D, 3DCueBench, and DA-2K with fewer generation calls than always generating.
  </details>

- **[Task-Utility IQA: Rethinking Image Quality Assessment in Embodied Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35905)**  `arXiv:2610.35905`  
  _Jirong Zha, Yanwei Liao, Yemin Wang, Xiangmin Yi, Siqi Peng, Yingfeng Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image quality assessment (IQA) underpins embodied imaging pipelines by judging whether visual quality satisfies downstream tasks, yet most existing methods learn task-agnostic scores aligned with generic human ratings on static benchmarks. This objective mismatches embodied and interactive settings, where image adequacy depends on task goals, context, and action requirements that shape an agent's decisions. We argue that IQA should shift from score regression to goal-conditioned judgment defined by embodied task utility. To this end, we propose an MLLM-based embodied IQA agent framework that reasons about task requirements, grounds quality evidence, and produces decision-facing assessments. A compact VisDrone evidence study shows that conventional perceptual metrics and generic MLLM-based IQA scores are weak proxies for downstream VLM task success, while a task-conditioned utility score better predicts whether visual input supports the target task. These findings support a task-utility-oriented reframing of IQA for embodied, explainable, and decision-aware visual perception.
  </details>

- **[ObjectTwin: Measuring the Asset Correspondence Gap for Capture-Anchored Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35904)**  `arXiv:2610.35904`  
  _Zhenghe Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object-wise reconstruction increasingly inserts generated meshes into captured scenes as editable proxies for observed objects. This use creates a requirement absent from isolated-asset scores and pose estimates: at the captured metric state, the proxy should reproduce calibrated observations of the same instance. We present ObjectTwin, a scan-grounded measurement protocol that freezes the instance, cameras, scan-projected support and recorded depth, metric anchor, and evaluation denominator, then replaces only the object representation. The resulting asset correspondence gap measures lost observation agreement relative to scan-derived visible geometry without treating the scan as complete physical ground truth. Across two indoor RGB-D capture domains and two generation backends, visible, anchored generated assets lose 0.302-0.497 MaskIoU under this replacement. Every tested scene-level estimate is positive, while same-input generator preference reverses across capture domains. Coverage and overlap-depth analyses locate disagreement in both visible support and surface placement; the evaluated conservative pose refinement does not systematically recover it. These results establish capture correspondence as a separately measurable interface between generated-asset quality and stronger physical or task-level evaluation.
  </details>

- **[VibeVideoBench: Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864)**  `arXiv:2610.35864`  
  _Muxi Diao, Wenbin Xu, Lele Yang, Xuyang Ma, Yuzhe Chen, Jiankang Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a rule-consistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox with no image or video generation model, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, scored by three VLM judge panels. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is a second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, a failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges' ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences.
  </details>

- **[Metric Scale for Insta360 Structure-from-Motion from the Camera's Embedded IMU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35554)**  `arXiv:2610.35554`  
  _Davide Cuttini, Diego Di Tommaso, Alessio Antoniacomi, Luca Simonetti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structure-from-motion (SfM) from the dual-fisheye video of a consumer 360-degree camera recovers geometry only up to a similarity transform; in the pipeline considered here, metric scale is obtained from smartphone ARKit or ARCore poses captured alongside the camera. This paper evaluates a self-contained alternative: the IMU embedded in the Insta360 X5 and X6, recorded at 1 kHz. The sensor is characterised (noise density, camera-IMU synchronisation, accelerometer scale factor, bias stability), the camera-IMU rotation and frame timing are estimated from the data, and the scale is recovered by a linear visual-inertial alignment over the SfM poses of a whole survey. The standard formulation, with gravity and accelerometer bias shared across the survey, overestimates the scale by 3.4% on average; estimating each contiguous segment independently and averaging removes the bias. On nine surveys with a co-mounted LiDAR scanner the inertial scale deviates from the LiDAR by 0.81% in mean absolute error, against 1.94% for the smartphone scale; on printed fiducial markers both agree within the 1% uncertainty of the target. On the reference trajectories of thirty sequences of a public benchmark, brought to scales of 1/40, 1 and 40, the estimator recovers the metric scale with a mean error of -0.07% and a standard deviation of 1.28%. On fifty production surveys where the smartphone is the only reference, the two scales agree to a standard deviation of 2.3%, the inertial scale being 0.3% lower on average; the difference is uncorrelated with run length, grows with the extent of the smartphone anchor, and cannot be attributed to either reference. An ablation over window construction and a comparison with monocular metric-depth networks complete the study.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Relay Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35219)**  `arXiv:2610.35219`  
  _Xu Liu, Jungang Qiao, Guikun Chen, Xiaojuan Qi, Wenguan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multi-agent collaboration (MAC) methods have primarily explored a group thinking collaboration topology, where agents independently generate solutions and aggregate their outputs. In this work, we investigate an alternative relay thinking topology, where reasoning ownership is dynamically transferred across agents, allowing subsequent agents to inherit and refine an evolving reasoning trajectory. We introduce Multi-Agent Confidence-guided Relay (MACR), a framework that enables dynamical relay collaboration among agents. The key challenge is determining when reasoning should be relayed and which agent should continue the process. method addresses this challenge through confidence-guided relay triggering and a bidding mechanism that selects the next agent based on its confidence. Experiments on four benchmarks show that method outperforms the strongest single-model strategies in accuracy, e.g., +3.3% on MathVista and +3.9% on MMStar, and remains on par with multi-agent strategies while consuming 6.0x less token overhead on HallusionBench and MMStar. Further analysis demonstrates that relay collaboration enables heterogeneous agents to progressively integrate complementary insights, combining heterogeneous capabilities across reasoning stages. Our source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MemVLN: Streaming Episodic Memory for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35066)**  `arXiv:2610.35066`  
  _Yan Wang, Jiangyong Huang, Siyuan Huang, Baoxiong Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow language instructions through 3D environments. Maintaining persistent episodic memory remains a challenge for VLM-based streaming agents in long-horizon navigation. We present MEMVLN, a streaming episodic memory framework that maintains a bounded bank of compressed perceptual features annotated with timesteps and action-derived relative poses, and produces compact memory tokens via cross-attention. We pair the proposed memory with a frozen pretrained geometry encoder. To learn the memory module, we design a masking-based trajectory summarization task with multi-granularity sub-trajectory decomposition, encouraging route reconstruction exclusively from episodic memory. Under navigation training restricted to the official R2R/RxR trajectories, MEMVLN achieves state-of-the-art performance on most reported metrics on standard VLN-CE benchmarks. Long-horizon analyses further characterize when episodic memory is useful. For physical evaluation, we collect 30 instruction-conditioned episodes in one physical scene, use 24 as demonstrations for post-training, and hold out six for testing. On the held-out episodes, MEMVLN improves success over the VLM baseline from 17% to 40%, and post-training further raises it to 47%.
  </details>

- **[CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34221)**  `arXiv:2610.34221`  
  _Xiangfei Sheng, Xiaofeng Pan, Leida Li, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Johan Wagemans, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than AIC existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.
  </details>

- **[WMEval: Evaluating Interactive World Models Grounded on Physical Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33894)**  `arXiv:2610.33894`  
  _Lanxiang Hu, Yixin Huang, Abhilash Shankarampeta, Zilin Dai, Yuyuan Wu, Kun Qian, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive world models hold immense potential for high-stakes applications in robotics, game engines, and immersive digital twins, yet existing benchmarks primarily focus on perceptual similarity rather than the physical-causal coherence essential for real-world utility. We introduce WMEval, a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates interactive world models against Unity-based physical simulations. By leveraging Unity as a high-fidelity ground-truth oracle, we shift evaluation beyond perceptual visual assessment toward the objective state-based verification required for downstream applications. Spanning 9 diverse scenes, WMEval assesses action controllability, scene and character consistency, prompt adherence, kinematics, fluid mechanics, physical causality, and lighting. By executing matched action sequences in both the simulator and the world model, WMEval enables evaluation at both the perceptual level and the physical-causal level, using simulator-derived states as reference. Experiments on state-of-the-art models reveal significant room for improvement in dimensions that remain hidden by standard perceptual metrics but are vital for real-world deployments. Our code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[UltraFlash: Accelerating Megapixel Visual Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33581)**  `arXiv:2610.33581`  
  _Phuc Lai, Anh Nguyen, Phong Nguyen, Anh Tran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Megapixel visual synthesis with latent diffusion models requires operating far beyond the resolutions at which these models are typically trained. Cascade refinement is a leading strategy for this setting: it starts from a base image at a native resolution and progressively upsamples it to the target resolution through multiple refinement stages. Each stage typically performs a pixel-space resolution transition followed by latent-space denoising. While prior acceleration efforts mainly focus on reducing denoising cost, we identify a complementary and underexplored bottleneck: the repeated movement of representations through the VAE. We call these operations pixel-space transitions. In cascade pipelines, intermediate latents are often decoded to RGB, resized, and re-encoded before refinement, while final latents are decoded patch by patch with dense overlap to suppress boundary artifacts. We propose UltraFlash, a transition-efficient approach that accelerates these VAE-heavy operations. UltraFlash has two components. Latent Hyperloop replaces intermediate RGB round trips with a learned latent-space shortcut that approximates the decode-resize-encode transformation without repeated VAE decoding and encoding. Sparse Patch-based Decoding accelerates final reconstruction by addressing patch seams caused by inconsistent GroupNorm statistics and reusing cached statistics across patches, enabling sparse VAE decoding with much smaller overlap. Integrated into a state-of-the-art cascade baseline, UltraFlash reduces 4K generation latency by approximately 2x while improving image quality. These results show that optimizing pixel-space transitions can improve both efficiency and fidelity, offering an effective route toward fast, high-quality megapixel visual synthesis.
  </details>

- **[Can One Hear Geometry? Gauge-Invariant Spectral Descriptors for Text-to-B-rep Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33484)**  `arXiv:2610.33484`  
  _Hongzhu Yi, Hao Liu, Tao Yu, Tiankun Yang, Yujia Yang, Zhenghao Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-3D solid retrieval faces a fundamental representation gap: while natural language describes intrinsic shapes, Boundary Representation (B-rep) entangles precise geometry with arbitrary variations in pose, scale, and boundary encoding conventions. Existing methods either discretize B-reps at the cost of analytic precision, or learn features from topological graphs without explicit invariance guarantees. We propose a text-to-B-rep retrieval framework based on geometric equivalence classes and spectral invariants, mathematically eliminating representation ambiguities prior to cross-modal alignment. Formalizing translation, rotation, reflection, uniform scaling, and contour re-indexing via group actions, we exploit the planar-profile-and-extrusion structure of engineering solids to construct a unified spectral descriptor across cross-sectional regions, contour boundaries, and extrusion depths. On public benchmarks, our method achieves state-of-the-art recall performance across all evaluation metrics.
  </details>

- **[Semantic-First: Temporal Scheduling in Flow Matching for Reliable Emotional Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33460)**  `arXiv:2610.33460`  
  _Zhenxuan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Affect-controlled image generation should change how a scene feels while preserving what the prompt specifies. We study the temporal allocation of stochastic exploration and policy optimization in flow-matching models as a means of controlling this balance. During RL training, our semantic-first schedule uses deterministic ordinary differential equation (ODE) sampling during the high-noise phase and confines stochastic differential equation (SDE) exploration and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to a late window. Controlled experiments separate exploration location, optimization location, and terminal-step sampling, showing distinct semantic benefits from late exploration and late updates. On FLUX.1-dev, semantic-first improves prompt faithfulness, independently evaluated affect accuracy, and human-rated semantic preservation and overall preference. The expanded semantic audit records 2.7% subject drift, while GRPO training time decreases by 41.1% relative to original full-path Flow-GRPO. Window-width experiments characterize the affect-semantic and computational trade-offs, and replication on SD3.5-Medium reproduces the scheduling advantage. These results establish denoising time as a practical control dimension for reliable emotional image generation.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Visual Language Models on Visual Grounding for Visually Impaired People](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33078)**  `arXiv:2610.33078`  
  _Fairooz Azim, Lydia Achour, Claudia Borg, Lina Rojas-Barahona_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual grounding (VG) in visual question answering (VQA) is a fundamental capability of Vision-Language Models (VLMs), enabling the detection of the most relevant region in an image to answer the question. This reflects the model's capability of multimodal comprehension, which is crucial when interacting with visually impaired people (VIP). Indeed, visual grounding, when applied to assistive scenarios, is not merely a technical feature, but rather a trust and safety requirement. In this work, we study visual grounding in VLMs for visually impaired people. To this end, we assess open-source VLMs on visual grounding using the VizWiz-VQA-Grounding dataset and challenge, which contains real-life images taken by VIP. We propose VG-SHAP, which extends an existing explainability framework to provide visual grounding explanations, allowing the evaluation of black-box VLMs. Our results on the VizWiz-VQA-Grounding challenge show that despite generating correct answers, VLMs struggle to ground in the proper region in the image. However, these results are constrained by the quality of the segmentation model of our explainability framework and further research is necessary.
  </details>

- **[K-CLUE: A Segmentation-to-Clustering Framework for Ki67 Cell Typing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32493)**  `arXiv:2610.32493`  
  _Ana Beatriz Vieira, Sara P. Oliveira, Diana Montezuma, João Fraga, Jaime S. Cardoso, Arlindo L. Oliveira_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ki67 is a key marker of tumor proliferation in breast cancer, but its quantification from immunohistochemistry images normally depends on supervised classifiers trained per dataset. We examine the role of frozen pretrained visual representations in annotation-free Ki67 cell typing. To this end, we use K-CLUE, a framework that segments individual nuclei, represents each nucleus with a pretrained encoder, reduces the resulting embeddings with principal component analysis, and separates Ki67-positive from Ki67-negative nuclei using k-means clustering, without task-specific training or manual annotations. Clustering the raw components is highly sensitive to the choice of encoder: across seven encoders, the Ki67-positive F1-score on matched MIST nuclei varies by 44 points, although Ki67 status remains linearly decodable from every representation, with supervised probe AUCs above 0.98. Analysis of the embedding spaces shows that clustering succeeds when DAB-related variation occupies the leading directions of variance. Whitening the retained principal components removes this dependence, reducing the spread across encoders to 1.00 F1-score point. Handcrafted color representations are also competitive, confirming that Ki67 status is strongly reflected in chromatic information, but they require an explicit choice of color space. In end-to-end evaluation, K-CLUE reaches F1-scores of 85.00% and 81.91% for Ki67-positive and Ki67-negative nuclei on MIST, and 79.59% and 81.16% on BCData, outperforming DeepLIIF and IHCScoreGAN on both datasets. These results show that pretrained representations can support annotation-free Ki67 typing when their variance structure is accounted for.
  </details>

- **[VisCurate: Evaluating Behavioral Equivalence of Visual Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30405)**  `arXiv:2610.30405`  
  _Md Farhan Ishraq, Md Farhan Ishmam, Reaz Hassan Joarder, Md Hasanul Kabir_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern vision-based agents have shifted toward skill-based problem-solving, where a skill is an executable image-to-image operation, e.g., cropping, thresholding, or edge detection. As these skill libraries grow, they accumulate duplicated, mislabeled, and defective skills. The signals normally used to curate them (a skill's name, description, or source code) say little about how the skill visually changes the image. We present VisCurate, a framework and benchmark that curates visual skill libraries from observed execution rather than from text. VisCurate runs each candidate skill pair on a shared probe battery and assigns one of six behavioral relations, each tied to a library edit that is safe to make. The benchmark injects seven defect types into a clean library at controlled rates, producing 300 reproducible instances with ground-truth relations and repairs. While text baselines accept up to five of six false merges on hard negatives, our verifier accepts none and recovers 51.2% of the pairs that are truly mergeable. On two real, uncurated libraries, VisCurate finds that 92% of the skills a package shares with its downstream copy are redundant, while execution confirms only 10 of 17 published equivalence claims. The curation problem also remains open, as the strongest of the nine automated curators recovers only about half of the repairs. We emphasize that visual skill libraries should be curated by behavior, not by description.
  </details>

- **[EgoHand: Monocular 3D Hand Reconstruction from Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30293)**  `arXiv:2610.30293`  
  _Chao-Yeh Chen, Tiffany Chen, Lin Duan, Tianyi Hu, Yu-Hsiang Huang, Umar Iqbal, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce EgoHand, a monocular 3D hand reconstruction model that achieves state-of-the-art performance on egocentric video. Central to our approach is the curation of a high-quality training corpus of 14 million images, combining synthetic data, in-the-wild video, and multi-camera motion capture to balance scene diversity and supervision quality. The model adopts SOMA-Hand as its parametric representation and processes 2D hand crops with a transformer encoder, incorporating camera cues and an auxiliary 2D branch to improve geometric consistency and image alignment. Zero-shot evaluations on HOT3D, ARCTIC, and H2O demonstrate strong out-of-domain generalization, with lower joint-position, root-translation, and acceleration errors than competing baselines at an inference latency of approximately 9.01 ms. A blinded user preference study further shows that participants favor EgoHand's reconstructed hand poses over those from Apple EgoDex and Ropedia Xperience-10M. We release the EgoHand model and source code under the OpenMDW license, allowing free commercial use of the model.
  </details>

- **[Look Beyond the Question: Preserve-Focus-Fuse for Whole-Slide Image Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29431)**  `arXiv:2610.29431`  
  _Xiangyu Li, Jincheng Li, Tianle Du, Yanheng Li, Haoxuan Che, Yuxin Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A question about a whole-slide image rarely names every morphological finding needed to answer it. Question-guided patch selection can therefore overlook supporting morphology, while slide-level compression can dilute focal evidence. Inspired by how pathologists combine broad slide review, question-directed examination, and integration of their findings, we introduce BEYONDPATH, a Preserve-Focus-Fuse framework for whole-slide image question answering. Preserve learns a question-agnostic Morphology Bank through answer supervision across multiple questions about each training slide. Focus retrieves questionspecific evidence from all foreground patches, including regions outside the Bank. Fuse integrates the preserved morphology at the retrieved evidence positions through Morphology-on-Visual-Evidence Fusion, producing a fixed 96-token visual prefix. On WSI-Bench, BEYONDPATH achieves an Avg-9 of 0.769 and leads all eight reported report-generation metrics using the same checkpoint. It also achieves 0.639 zero-shot overall accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB. Controlled ablations show complementary benefits from morphology preservation and question-directed retrieval, and support cross-question supervision and fusion at the evidence positions. These results support looking beyond the question's explicit cues: preserving broader slide morphology while retaining the ability to seek evidence for each question.
  </details>

- **[Procedural Exposure Bias in Skill-Augmented Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26062)**  `arXiv:2610.26062`  
  _Qing Yang, Yuqi Xiong, Pengcheng Huang, Zhipeng Xu, Zhenghao Liu, Zulong Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External skills provide language-model agents with reusable procedural knowledge, yet how models use such knowledge after it is supplied remains poorly understood. We identify Procedural Exposure Bias (PEB), the tendency of language models to increasingly favor an action as its associated procedural pattern is repeatedly exposed in a skill, even when the interaction state and action relevance remain unchanged. We find that PEB strongly depends on how procedural knowledge is represented: presenting the same content visually substantially attenuates exposure-induced shifts in action preference relative to textual presentation. Further analysis shows that this attenuation primarily arises from visual encoding rather than structured layout. Although repeated procedural signals remain recoverable from Visual representations, they exert a weaker influence on downstream action prediction, distinguishing signal accessibility from its use as action evidence. This effect also extends to executable action selection, where increasing exposure more strongly favors the corresponding action under Text than Visual. Layer-wise analyses show that exposure-dependent action evidence becomes more pronounced toward the model output, with stronger evidence under Text, while activation interventions reveal architecture-specific roles of internal components in transmitting or counteracting the preference shift. Complete-agent evaluations across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and WebShop further show that Visual skills achieve higher macro-average task success than Text skills for all four evaluated models. These findings establish skill representation as an important factor governing how procedural knowledge is translated into agent decisions. All code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CertEx: Exact Visual Retrieval at Scale by Certified Exclusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21325)**  `arXiv:2610.21325`  
  _Dongfang Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exact $k$-nearest-neighbor (kNN) search over image descriptors and embeddings underlies visual retrieval, near-duplicate detection, the evaluation of learned representations, and increasingly the memory of LLM agents. However, exhaustive search computes every distance at full precision, whereas approximate indexes return recall below one. We observe that exact search only needs a proof that each excluded vector cannot be a neighbor. Because such a proof only has to be conservative, it can be checked in low precision, much as the retina examines the whole scene at low resolution. We call this principle certified exclusion and realize it in an exact kNN method for GPUs, which we call CertEx. Specifically, CertEx stores a lower-bounding projection of each vector in half precision and folds a rounding certificate into the same tensor-core dot product, so that only the few vectors that the test keeps need exact distances. We prove that CertEx returns the exact kNN of every query. On sixteen datasets with up to 100M vectors, CertEx is exact for every query at 7.1$\times$ to 18.0$\times$ the throughput of the fastest cuVS search on one H100 (8.8$\times$ to 11.8$\times$ on an A100) and outperforms quantized and graph indexes whenever every query must be exact.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: A Subspecialty Foundation Model for Intraoperative Central Nervous System Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16914)**  `arXiv:2610.16914`  
  _Xing Liu, Yifan Guo, Hongtai Lv, Lei Lou, Yuyi Tan, Jiang Du, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intraoperative frozen-section diagnosis of central nervous system (CNS) lesions guides surgical decisions but is challenged by artefacts, diagnostic complexity and time constraints. Most pathology foundation models have limited exposure to CNS frozen tissue. Here we present FOCUS, a subspecialty foundation model pretrained on 27,930 frozen-section whole-slide images enriched for CNS tissue. FOCUS ranked first across all 11 diagnostic and 12 biomarker-prediction tasks in the internal test cohort, achieving mean AUCs of 0.969 and 0.877, respectively. This advantage was retained in the temporal and external test cohorts. We further organized a WHO-aligned diagnostic hierarchy, where FOCUS also outperformed comparator models. In a reader study, FOCUS assistance enabled junior readers to approach or exceed unassisted senior readers in hierarchical diagnostic accuracy and granularity. More importantly, FOCUS assistance enabled readers to correctly identify a mean of 63.0% of initially missed lymphoma cases (73.2% among junior readers), potentially avoiding unnecessary extensive resection. These findings highlight the potential of FOCUS to provide clinically actionable support for intraoperative CNS pathology and inform surgical decisions.
  </details>

- **[Corrupted but Correct: Why Vision-Language Models Lie to Themselves Internally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03445)**  `arXiv:2610.03445`  
  _Arun Josephraj Arokiaraj, Chenyi Huang, Zekun Wu, Adriano Koshiyama_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A targeted adversarial perturbation can drive a vision-language model's (VLM's) teacher-forced training loss for a fixed target caption to near zero, yet the same model, allowed to generate freely, produces the original, correct description with no trace of the target. We call this dissociation the train/inference gap, and give it a precise mechanistic account on Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct using a controlled two-stage PGD attack on 200 held-out COCO images. First, we show that image-level pixel statistics, including a correctly re-implemented, texture-based attackability measure from the CNN robustness literature, have essentially no predictive power over which images are corrupted (best predictor r=-0.050, p=0.484; ridge regression R^2=0.069). Second, using the logit lens, we localise the gap to a single autoregressive step: the rank of the target token, conditioned on the correct first token already being generated, is fixed at exactly 3,488 out of 152,064 vocabulary entries for every image and every condition, with zero variance. Third, tracking target-token rank across all 28 LLM decoder layers reveals that the visual encoder corrupts every image's representation by a comparable margin regardless of eventual outcome, but the language model decoder then differentially arbitrates: amplifying the corrupted signal for susceptible images and actively suppressing it, past its clean-image baseline, for resistant ones (p<0.001, rank-biserial r=0.579). A linear probe on the merger hidden state separates these two outcomes with AUC=0.858, though we flag a circularity concern in this estimate. Together these results argue that adversarial robustness in autoregressive VLMs is substantially a property of the language decoder's prior, not the visual encoder, with direct implications for where faithfulness evaluations and defenses for deployed VLM systems should be targeted.
  </details>

- **[OmniSeek: Native Tool Integration for Multi-turn Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02181)**  `arXiv:2610.02181`  
  _Haibo Wang, Jiteng Mu, Jialu Li, Jingru Yi, Yuanjun Xiong, Jianming Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OmniSeek, an agentic framework that transforms an Omni Large Language Model (Omni-LLM) into an active, multi-turn reasoning agent with native tool use. Rather than passively processing an entire audio-visual sequence in a single forward pass, OmniSeek makes evidence acquisition part of the reasoning process: it dynamically decides whether to look or listen, and over which temporal window, to retrieve sparse but critical evidence across different modalities within long contexts. Through an iterative multi-turn protocol, the retrieved raw audio or visual segments are appended back into the context to support subsequent reasoning. To cold-start this capability, we build a data engine that synthesizes OmniTraj-170K, a corpus of multi-hop Chain-of-Thought trajectories with interleaved audio and visual evidence. We first supervise the model on these trajectories to instill multi-turn tool-use behavior, and then further optimize the policy via a two-stage reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Moreover, we introduce an Audio-Visual Necessity objective that explicitly rewards successful trajectories whose reasoning depends on both modalities, discouraging single-modality shortcuts. Extensive experiments across a wide range of benchmarks demonstrate that OmniSeek learns adaptive cross-modal evidence seeking and consistently improves audio-visual reasoning performance.
  </details>

- **[SuperMotion: Source-Preserving Denoising for Text-Driven Human Motion Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01517)**  `arXiv:2610.01517`  
  _Fa-Ting Hong, Peter Wonka_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-driven human motion editing aims to realize a requested change while preserving compatible source content. Existing diffusion editors rely largely on learned conditioning for preservation of the unedited part, yet their outputs can lose temporal detail as denoising proceeds. We propose the \textbf{Source-Preserving Denoising framework (SuperMotion)}, which explicitly reuses the source at each reverse step for source preservation. We first align the source motion to the output timeline and predict a preservation gate that controls reuse across frames and feature dimensions. A clean-space source anchor then utilizes the learned preservation gate to blend the predicted clean motion with the aligned source and passes the corrected estimate directly to the sampling posterior. Because the aligned source is a realized motion rather than a regression output, the anchor injects sample-level temporal detail that a reconstruction-trained denoiser tends to smooth away. To learn effective source reuse, we supervise the anchored estimate against the editing target and match its second temporal differences through a temporal high-frequency loss. These objectives require no explicit edit masks. Extensive experiments show that SuperMotion improves editing accuracy, reaching 33.20\% full-pool R@1 on MotionFix, while reducing temporal-detail attenuation and preserving motion dynamics as it realizes the requested changes. Ablations confirm that the learned preservation gate is responsible for the gain and that it reuses the source to retain the unedited content properly.
  </details>

- **[CLeaR: A Unified Framework for Resolving the Leakage-Degradation Dilemma in Style Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38136)**  `arXiv:2609.38136`  
  _Teng Zhou, Yunhao Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Style transfer aims to render target content in the style of a reference image, but existing methods often suffer from content leakage, where objects, layouts, or semantics from the style reference appear in the generated output. Although prior data-driven and training-free methods can reduce leakage, they often face a leakage-degradation dilemma: stronger content suppression may weaken style fidelity, while richer style preservation may reintroduce unwanted reference content. We identify this dilemma across the full style-transfer pipeline, including feature separation, feature-space grounding, and diffusion generation. To address these issues, we propose CLeaR, a training-free framework for content-leakage-resistant style transfer. CLeaR first uses Orthogonal Subspace Projection to define content-reduced style targets in each vision foundation model (VFM) feature space. It then performs Ensemble Inversion, which optimizes a shared pixel-space style anchor satisfying style constraints across multiple VFMs. Finally, Energy-Guided Calibration maintains style alignment during diffusion sampling by steering the denoising trajectory toward the ensemble-defined style manifold. We further provide a theoretical analysis showing that the style-anchor estimation error decreases with the number of VFMs. Experiments on StyleBench demonstrate that CLeaR improves style alignment, reduces content leakage, and achieves better LLM-as-Judge evaluation compared with existing methods. The code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[GAUGE: Group-Wise View-Inconsistency Rectification for Feed-Forward 4D Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32596)**  `arXiv:2609.32596`  
  _Zhuoqian Feng, Weixing Chen, Ziliang Chen, Yang Liu, Liang Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward models regress dense 3D point trajectories directly from monocular video, yet the residual after global alignment is substantial and lacks a structural explanation. Measured on dynamic query points across models and datasets, the error concentrates along the view direction, while the scale correction each motion group requires differs. The predicted displacement direction nevertheless supports reliable grouping, with a median angle far below the 90° random baseline. The systematic part of the residual is therefore a family of radial degrees of freedom per motion group, along directions 2D observations cannot constrain. We call it group-wise view inconsistency. We present GAUGE (Group-wise Adaptive Unsupervised Gauge Estimation), a training-free and model-agnostic post-hoc module. It recovers motion groups from direction consistency and spatial connectivity, then estimates a per-frame radial scale and group-level translation from 1% to 5% metric anchors, four degrees of freedom per group and frame. On dynamic query points of eight trackers, including D4RT, 4RC and SM4RT, our correction lowers endpoint error by 15.1% to 62.6% over the uncorrected predictions, while spending the same anchors on gradient fine-tuning improves the same models by only -1.1% to 15.2%. Code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DIP-CAD: A Design-Intent-Preserving Agent Harness for Parametric CAD Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22688)**  `arXiv:2609.22688`  
  _Xi Cheng, Chenxi Zhai, Hang Cheng, Mingyu Fan, Pingfa Feng, Long Zeng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric CAD modeling requires not only matching the target shape, but also preserving design intent under parameter edits. However, most existing methods focus on improving static shape generation accuracy, while the dependencies that govern later edits are often overlooked. To tackle this issue, we present DIP-CAD, a design-intent-preserving agent harness for generating parametric CAD models from images. DIP-CAD combines vision-language model reasoning with CAD kernel operations to establish dependencies that encode the design intent. In parametric CAD, these dependencies are mainly established through geometry references in modeling commands and constraints in parametric sketches. At the command level, ID-based explicit geometry reference binds modeling commands to selected faces, edges, or vertices. At the sketch level, a local-coordinate bridge ensures accurate sketch placement, providing the basis for correctly establishing sketch constraints. Constraints among sketch primitives and constraints to external projected geometry further preserve both internal sketch relationships and dependencies on existing geometry. To provide a more comprehensive data foundation for parametric CAD modeling tasks, we introduce the Geometry Explicit Reference Dataset (GERD), whose structured data format links stepwise modeling commands to intermediate CAD models through explicit geometry references. On GERD-EVL and a DeepCAD test subset, DIP-CAD improves mIoU for static model generation by 11.1% and 5.6%, respectively. In parameter-editing evaluations, the corresponding mIoU improvements are 7.0% and 4.2%. Case studies further demonstrate how parameter edits drive shape updates while preserving design intent.
  </details>

- **[MUMINS: Metadata-conditioned Uncertainty-aware Medical Image Next-state Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17169)**  `arXiv:2609.17169`  
  _Anna Oliveras, Roger Marí, Rafael Redondo, Oriol Guardià, Cynthia Ifeyinwa Ugwu, Ana Tost, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting anatomical changes such as tumor growth and neurodegeneration is a challenging generative vision task. Morphological evolution is subtle relative to static anatomy, highly patient-specific, and inherently stochastic. Existing methods struggle with several issues: deterministic networks ignore biological stochasticity, while standard diffusion models require computationally prohibitive multi-pass sampling to quantify uncertainty. We propose MUMINS (Metadata-conditioned Uncertainty-aware Medical Image Next-state Synthesis), an efficient diffusion framework that jointly diffuses a baseline scan and its follow-up residual, summed to synthesize the follow-up scan, while concurrently predicting a spatial uncertainty map, in a single reverse diffusion process. Conditioned on the time interval and relevant metadata, it preserves fine-grained anatomy by dynamically re-injecting the baseline as a soft anchor at every denoising step, and a negative-log-likelihood head learns the uncertainty map to explicitly flag error-prone regions. Designed without organ-specific heuristics, the same architecture is reused across anatomies via separate, dataset-specific retraining. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that dataset-specific retraining of MUMINS matches or outperforms dedicated, domain-specific state-of-the-art methods on lung CT (PNG) and brain MRI (OASIS-3). Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Does Attention-Guided Masking Really Help Object Discovery in Object-Centric Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15187)**  `arXiv:2609.15187`  
  _Youliang Tao, Yanhua Han, Bin Zhao, Juho Kannala, Joni Pajarinen, Rongzhen Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object-Centric Learning (OCL) aims to decompose images into objects without human annotations. A major family of mainstream methods uses Slot Attention to aggregate image features into object-level representations and then from them reconstructs masked image content, i.e., Random Masking (RM), to provide self-supervision. The recent method DIAS simply masks image patches at uniform randomness yet achieves competitive object discovery accuracy. Since attention during aggregation already possesses object discovery ability, we explore using it to develop a better image patch masking strategy, i.e., Attention Guided Masking (AGM), thereby providing better self-supervision. Results on six recognized datasets show that AGM does not always outperform RM. Under unconditional slot initialization, AGM substantially improves background segmentation on datasets with realistic textures (COCO and VOC); Regardless of conditional or unconditional slot initialization and across datasets, foreground object discovery remains comparable or decreases. We suggest peer researchers in the OCL community that attempts to exploit internal attention semantics to improve OCL with masked decoding are risky. Our source code, model checkpoints and evaluation logs are available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OctWorld: Long-Range World-Consistent Video Generation with Octree-Based 3D Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03919)**  `arXiv:2609.03919`  
  _Zelong Lv, Sicheng Xu, Jianfeng Xiang, Ruicheng Wang, Yue Dong, Yu Deng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OctWorld, a video diffusion framework with persistent 3D memory for generating explorable, world-consistent, and high-fidelity visual scenes. Given a single image, OctWorld performs stable autoregressive world generation along user-specified camera trajectories. We focus on long-range generation, characterized by extended camera paths and wide viewpoint coverage, where preserving spatial consistency is particularly challenging when previously generated regions are revisited. To address this problem, we introduce OctMap, an extensible and spatially adaptive 3D memory that progressively fuses generated visual observations and their corresponding depth maps into a global representation. OctMap employs TSDF fusion within a dynamic sparse octree whose spatial resolution adapts to image evidence. This design preserves geometric and appearance details across diverse scene scales while maintaining low memory overhead. Experiments demonstrate that OctWorld generates long-range, spatially consistent videos and outperforms prior methods on both existing benchmarks and challenging long-range generation settings. OctMap also provides clear advantages over point-based caches and fixed-resolution TSDF volumes. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ToPO: Token-Oriented Preference Optimization for Text-to-Image Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03688)**  `arXiv:2609.03688`  
  _Juntao Xu, Shihong Li, Hoi Fan Au, Ning Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in text-to-image generation, yet aligning their outputs with human preferences remains challenging. Diffusion-DPO learns from image preference pairs, but their global labels do not specify how supervision should be allocated across spatial locations and denoising timesteps. The semantic roles of prompt tokens are also not explicitly considered when assigning these local weights. To address these limitations, we propose Token-Oriented Preference Optimization (ToPO), which builds a token-conditioned spatial-temporal weighting map for each pair. A frozen reference denoiser obtains spatial and temporal weights from local noise-prediction error differences under shared noise, while preferred-branch cross-attention links spatial evidence to content tokens. The map reweights Diffusion-DPO and supports an auxiliary pixel-midpoint ordering loss. It requires neither local preference annotations nor a separately trained reward model. Extensive experiments across multiple preference and compositional evaluations show that ToPO achieves state-of-the-art performance among the compared methods with only 500 training updates, demonstrating the potential of token-conditioned preference allocation for high-quality diffusion alignment within a limited update budget.
  </details>
