For such tasks, the central question is not expressivity--a dense neural network may contain the desired map--but finite-data identification, extrapolation, and certification. We introduce the structure gap: the finite-resource gap between a method that receives the governing structure and a learner that receives only examples. The strongest useful form of the claim is asymptotic rather than absolute: structure-agnostic learning may approach a structured solver as coverage grows, but every finite dataset can leave residual uncertainty that the structure resolves by construction. We prove this exactly for a Gaussian inverse problem: the known-operator Wiener solver attains Bayes risk, while the dense empirical learner in the correct linear class has persistent expected excess risk. We also prove a cell-coverage lower bound and an unobserved-orbit non-identifiability result. Controlled experiments in known-operator inversion, sparse recovery, and graph reachability with size extrapolation instantiate the mechanisms. When validated structure is available, it can constrain the hypothesis space before learning begins, leaving learning to address components the structure does not specify.
  </details>

- **[Orthogonal and Parametrized Multi-Attribute Steering of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37517)**  `arXiv:2610.37517`  
  _Remy Ogasawara, Cullen Anderson, Narmeen Fatimah Oozeer, Foad Namjoo, Amir Abdullah, Jens Kristian Refsgaard Schou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering offers an effective and efficient way to modify an LLM's behavior at inference time. However, it remains unclear how to apply steering such that the signal is consistently strong and the steering strength has a universally interpretable meaning. We address this first by identifying the most steerable interval for each steering vector and rescaling the steering range accordingly. Building on this, we introduce a more interpretable method for steering, called parameterized steering which instead of pushing activations in a direction, it sets them at the desired trait strength. Next, with multi-trait steering, we find that naively combining vectors causes them to interfere with one another. In response, we develop a method to orthogonalize the vectors; it decouples these traits, allowing a model to exhibit one behavior without inducing the others, thus enabling multiple traits to be steered simultaneously.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable Meta-Policies for Neural Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37451)**  `arXiv:2610.37451`  
  _Raban Emunds, Jannis Blüml, Quentin Delfosse, Kristian Kersting_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep Reinforcement Learning remains difficult to interpret and align due to the black-box nature of neural networks. While (neuro-)symbolic approaches improve transparency by incorporating symbolic reasoning, applying them directly to low-level actions results in overly complex, unreadable policies. We introduce NEXUS, a hierarchical RL framework that resolves this tension by combining neural skills with symbolic meta-policies. By shifting transparent reasoning to high-level skills rather than atomic actions, NEXUS drastically reduces symbolic complexity without sacrificing environment performance. Object-centric observations enable high-level reasoning and the design of reward functions and meta-policies by interaction with LLMs. We demonstrate that NEXUS agents learn disentangled skills, are interpretable regarding high-level goals, are better aligned to goals described in the game manual, less prone to reward hacking and more robust to game simplifications than deep RL. Finally, we isolate and evaluate the impact of different meta-policy architectures (neural, symbolic, neuro-symbolic) on environmental return and aligned task performance.
  </details>

- **[CRISP: Co-designing Pruning and Scheduling for Efficient MoE Inference on Edge Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37442)**  `arXiv:2610.37442`  
  _Congwei Zhang, Youming Tao, Yifei Zou, Yu Yang, Ruirui Zhang, Xiaoyu Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) inference on memory-constrained edge platforms is often dominated by expert-weight movement rather than computation. Although each token activates only a small subset of experts, routing is input-dependent, so all experts must remain accessible and memory movement dominates latency. Existing approaches such as uniform pruning and expert dropping overlook two properties of MoE execution: expert combinations are highly skewed in frequency, and experts contribute asymmetrically within each combination. We present CRISP, a combination-aware framework that co-designs pruning and runtime scheduling for efficient MoE serving on memory-constrained edge platforms. CRISP reorders neurons by importance so one stored expert can support multiple execution widths, assigns differentiated execution to hot, warm, and cold combinations based on frequency and expert role, and exploits heterogeneous widths to overlap expert loading with computation. Across Mixtral-8x7B and DeepSeek-MoE-16B, CRISP improves throughput by up to $2.1\times$ at the same memory budget while limiting accuracy loss to 3.6\% relative to full-width inference. Our results highlight that efficient edge MoE serving requires jointly optimizing which expert capacity is preserved and how expert execution is scheduled.
  </details>

- **[When Visual Evidence Is Insufficient: How Instructions, Post-Training, and Internal Activations Shape Refusal in Multimodal Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37430)**  `arXiv:2610.37430`  
  _Ananth K Suresh, Aveen Dayal, Gouthaman KV, Aditya Pola, Vineeth N Balasubramanian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For reliable deployment, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) should recognize when an image provides insufficient evidence to answer a question and withhold unsupported answers. Yet refusal alone is not necessarily evidence of such recognition: models may refuse because of unrelated safety constraints or without identifying the missing evidence. We study how refusal on visually unanswerable questions is shaped by three sources of control: inference-time instructions, post-training, and internal activation interventions. Across three MLLMs on the MoHoBench benchmark, we systematically manipulate the meaning and placement of refusal instructions, compare supervised and preference-based post-training, and intervene on a refusal-related activation direction derived from naturally answered and refused examples. We find that refusal is highly sensitive to both the meaning and placement of instructions, with model-specific changes at the sample level that are obscured by aggregate refusal rates. Evidence-aware and general refusal instructions have stronger effects than an intentionally mismatched safety instruction. Post-training produces larger increases in refusal, but can also induce responses that combine refusal language with attempted answers. In contrast, activation intervention produces smaller yet transferable changes across prompt conditions, without evidence of indiscriminate refusal on tested visually answerable samples. These findings show that refusal is jointly shaped by external instructions, post-training, and internal representations, highlighting the need to disentangle evidence recognition from a model's learned propensity to refuse.
  </details>

- **[Bayes-Adaptive MDPs for Rollout Allocation to Enhance GRPO Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37429)**  `arXiv:2610.37429`  
  _Faeze Moradi Kalarde, Armin Toroghi, Nam Ho Koh, Doo Hwan Hwang, Seungjin Kim, Kee-Eung Kim, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has demonstrated strong performance in LLM post-training. However, it allocates a uniform rollout budget to every training question, ignoring variations in question difficulty. As a result, highly difficult questions may receive too few rollouts to generate informative positive reward signals, limiting their contribution to training, while moderately difficult questions often receive more rollouts than necessary. In this work, we formulate the problem of difficulty-aware rollout budget allocation to maximize training utility under the same total rollout budget as standard GRPO. We propose AdaRoll-GRPO, which reformulates rollout allocation as a sequential decision-making problem and leverages a Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process (MDP) to learn an adaptive allocation policy. The learned policy is computed offline and applied during GRPO training without introducing additional online optimization overhead. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning datasets and LLM architectures demonstrate that AdaRoll-GRPO consistently improves GRPO training performance under a fixed rollout budget, highlighting the effectiveness of adaptive rollout allocation for GRPO in LLM post-training.
  </details>

- **[How Many and How Much? Contrastive Learning with Negative Samples for Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37389)**  `arXiv:2610.37389`  
  _Sooho Moon, Yunyong Ko_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph embedding (KGE), a fundamental technique for knowledge-centric Web and KG-augmented AI applications, is commonly trained through contrastive learning with negative samples to learn discriminative representations. Despite its empirical success, two crucial questions regarding negative samples remain underexplored: (Q1) how many negative samples should be used for each positive sample and (Q2) how much should each negative sample contribute to learning? To answer these questions, we propose RADAR, a novel contrastive learning framework for KGE models with two effective strategies, respectively: (1) Risk-aware negative budget allocation quantifies false-negative sampling risk by modeling an open-world KG as a stochastic partial observation of its closed-world counterpart and adaptively assigns a negative budget to each positive sample based on its risk; (2) Distribution-calibrated negative weighting assigns negative weights by calibrating the weighting strength for each positive based on two complementary characteristics of the negative-score distribution: dispersion and concentration. Extensive experiments on 3 real-world KGs with 4 KGE models reveal that RADAR achieves the best KGE performance on average across all model--KG combinations and reduces sampled false negatives by over 90% compared with conventional fixed-budget methods.
  </details>

- **[Channel Selection for Mixture-of-Experts Decoding Needs No Calibration Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37386)**  `arXiv:2610.37386`  
  _Geunhyoung Kim, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.
  </details>

- **[Euclidean Crowding-Driven Adaptive Euclidean-Hyperbolic Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37373)**  `arXiv:2610.37373`  
  _Hao Chen, Jian Sun, Chuan Shi, Hongying Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperbolic graph representation learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for modeling hierarchical data. Unlike Euclidean space, which exhibits polynomial volume growth, hyperbolic space grows exponentially with radius, naturally accommodating tree-like structures without the geometric distortion known as Euclidean crowding. Despite strong empirical performance, the theoretical mechanisms underlying hyperbolic embeddings remain poorly understood. In this work, we provide a geometric quantifcation research for Euclidean crowding, a generalization-theoretic explanation for the superiority of hyperbolic space on hierarchical data, and further develop an adaptive Euclidean-hyperbolic fusion learning framework toward bridging this gap. Specifically, we \textbf{first} establish a geometric quantification of Euclidean crowding by analyzing layer-wise minimum separation in node embeddings, proving that fixed-dimensional Euclidean embeddings of tree-like graphs inevitably exhibit layer-wise crowding that leads to weaker margin-based generalization bounds. \textbf{Second}, we provide a generalization-bound for the superiority of hyperbolic geometry on hierarchical data. \textbf{Third}, we propose EH-Fusion, a geometry-adaptive framework that lifts Euclidean GNN representations into hyperbolic space via exponential mapping and adaptively fuses both geometries based on node-level neighborhood expansion patterns. Extensive experiments validate our theoretical insights and demonstrate the effectiveness of EH-Fusion on benchmark datasets.
  </details>

- **[TRIDENT-Seq: Profile-Matched Prefix-Future Contrast for Label-Efficient Payment Risk Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37365)**  `arXiv:2610.37365`  
  _Weiyu Han, Haoyu Jiang, Jie Sheng, Ruobing Zuo, Huilin He, Ze Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ant International accumulates large volumes of transaction history across users and payment businesses. Confirmed fraud and chargeback outcomes, by contrast, are scarce, delayed, and specific to each business. A single encoder pretrained on unlabeled histories could serve all businesses, but only if pretraining captures the behavior that separates risk. Coarse activity differences can make contrastive pretraining on transaction logs too easy: transaction volume, amount, and timing can distinguish a history's continuation from arbitrary candidates without modeling how payment resources are used. We present TRIDENT-Seq, a transaction foundation model trained with Prefix-Future Contrast (PFC). PFC matches each prefix to a summary of its subsequent behavior among candidates with similar activity profiles; activity prediction and dispersion regularization act on the encoder output. Across three production payment businesses, the pretrained encoder improves average precision (AP) and recall at a 0.5% intervention budget over architecture-matched scratch training in all six business-budget settings. With a fixed validation set, it exceeds full-label scratch training in mean AP and recall using 10% of the training labels on Wallet and 25% on Shopping and SME. Pretraining only on another business's histories still helps. Replacing profile-matched candidates with random ones cuts recall from 24.62% to 5.64%. In Ant International's deployment evaluation, recorded amount-based chargeback and overall loss rates are 37.7% and 37.9% lower than those of the previous XGBoost-based system under the same intervention budget.
  </details>

- **[Normative Deviation Modeling for EEG-based Depression Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37338)**  `arXiv:2610.37338`  
  _Wan Jiang, Jingqi Cheng, Hui Zhang, Xu Zhao, Xiaojing Chen, Shengeng Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web-enabled digital health services are expanding access to physiological monitoring and mental health screening. However, electroencephalography (EEG)-based major depressive disorder (MDD) detection remains challenging across heterogeneous data sources, where individual variability and acquisition differences may affect disease-related representations. In this work, we investigate the use of healthy normative references for EEG-based MDD detection. Specifically, structural and distributional reference models are independently trained using healthy control (HC) recordings to characterize departures from healthy EEG patterns. We develop two diagnostic approaches that incorporate normative deviations at different representation levels. Scalar-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (S-NDD) summarizes structural and distributional deviations into scalar scores and integrates them with deep EEG representations. Feature-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (F-NDD) retains dimension-wise residuals and combines them with deep EEG representations through attention-based aggregation and auxiliary supervision. We also employ target HC-assisted score calibration for cross-source evaluation without updating the trained models. Experiments on two public EEG datasets examine both approaches under subject-independent within-source and bidirectional cross-source settings. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of normative deviation-guided diagnosis across different evaluation settings.
  </details>

- **[CoS-VQ: Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37336)**  `arXiv:2610.37336`  
  _Zukang Xu, Zhixiong Zhao, Xing Hu, Zhe Jiang, Xin Si, Dawei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) require substantial memory, motivating low-bit weight quantization for efficient deployment. Existing vector quantization (VQ) methods often improve accuracy by increasing the effective coding dimension, which can complicate independent lookup and local codeword updates. We propose CoS-VQ (Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization), which preserves low-dimensional codewords while jointly optimizing hard indices and their shared scale across positions. The key challenge is scale-induced coupling: the optimal scale depends on the entire codeword combination and, in turn, changes candidate rankings across positions, so pointwise updates can stall even after scale re-optimization. CoS-VQ collects candidates at multiple probe scales and constructs a tangent separable upper bound for the residual-aware quadratic objective. By reducing candidate comparisons to lower envelopes of lines over positive scales, it identifies finitely many representative intervals for joint index--scale updates without exhaustively enumerating codeword combinations. On Qwen3.8-27B, CoS-VQ outperforms QTIP and LLVQ on the evaluated language modeling, coding, and reasoning benchmarks. Ablations from the same initialization further show larger gains from joint updates than from scale-only refinement, highlighting joint selection as an alternative to increasing codeword dimensionality. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WCC: A Learning-Based Framework for Deciding When to Apply Cooperative Coevolution in Large-Scale Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37329)**  `arXiv:2610.37329`  
  _Wenjie Qiu, Hepei Li, Jianfeng Wang, Jun Zhang, Yue-Jiao Gong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative Coevolution (CC) and Non-Decomposition Algorithms (NDAs) are two primary approaches for large-scale global optimization (LSGO). Although both have achieved success, they exhibit markedly different performance across problem types and each faces approach-specific limitations, leaving the question of "when to use CC" unresolved. We address this gap with WCC, a novel learning-based framework that dynamically decides when to deploy CC in LSGO. WCC employs a streamlined design for efficient adaptive learning. We formulate the decision as a Markov decision process, designing a targeted feature set, a robust reward, and a tailored network architecture to provide the agent with rich information about the problem state and optimization progress. Our auto-regressive policy enables WCC to perform fine-grained, dynamic switching between CC and NDAs while conditionally allocating the computational budget without expert specifications. WCC shows strong competitiveness and superior generalization across problems, optimization horizons, and baseline optimizers. It realizes a "train-once and reuse" paradigm under a low budget, significantly curbing LSGO cost. Additionally, in-depth analyses yield insights not captured by existing theory, offering practical guidance on when and how to effectively deploy CC.
  </details>

- **[Structured Representation Probing for Risk Anticipation in End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37299)**  `arXiv:2610.37299`  
  _Jiawei Liang, Tianrui Lou, Le Yang, Xianghao Jiao, Wei Wang, Xiaochun Cao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end driving policies form structured internal representations of traffic, ego behavior, and road constraints. These representations offer a monitoring interface for anticipating failures, complementing risk signals from trajectories, controls, and manually designed features. We introduce the Structured Representation Probe (SRP), a lightweight monitor that reads actor, ego, and map representations from a frozen policy. SRP learns a separate linear risk direction for each source and aggregates element scores through cardinality-normalized log-mean-exp pooling, retaining sensitivity to salient evidence while reducing dependence on token count. Event-window supervision links the readout to upcoming hard failures, and auxiliary event type heads jointly train the source directions across failure modes. Online alerting uses a single generic score without modifying the driving policy. Bench2Drive experiments on DriveTransformer, UniAD, and VAD show higher frame-level PR-AUC and ROC-AUC than the evaluated runtime baselines. On DriveTransformer at a horizon of 2 seconds, ROC-AUC reaches 0.833 versus 0.715 for temporal consistency, with 2.67% readout runtime overhead. The trained probes also retain the ability to distinguish impending failures on new routes.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Inference of Expensive Simulators: Survey and Comparison of Design Choices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37284)**  `arXiv:2610.37284`  
  _Šimon Soldát, Václav Šmídl_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a survey of approaches to Bayesian inference with simulator forward models, focusing on settings with prohibitively expensive simulators. In this domain, Bayesian optimization-based methods have emerged as the most promising approach. We unify existing methods into a general framework, enabling consistent reasoning about shared algorithmic structure across the fragmented literature. Within this framework, we identify the primary design choices and perform a systematic empirical comparison of their effects on inference efficiency in terms of required simulation budget.A key result of our study is that inference efficiency is often dominated by the choice of surrogate-model response variable, as it directly governs the complexity of the modeled function. Specifically, we demonstrate that the prevalent scalar log-likelihood is a poor response variable, as it discards crucial information. Based on this insight, we introduce the concept of custom \emph{proxy variables}: transformations of simulator outputs engineered to retain more information and provide smoother response surfaces, substantially improving inference efficiency over standard response variables.We provide actionable guidance and a numerically robust Julia package to facilitate practical adoption of Bayesian optimization for simulator inverse problems.
  </details>

- **[Sharpness-Aware Muon: Matrix-Aware Optimization with Momentum-Guided Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37225)**  `arXiv:2610.37225`  
  _Zhiwei Zhai, Wenjing Yan, Ying-Jun Angela Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon is a matrix-aware optimizer that combines momentum with orthogonalization to produce structured update directions with strong empirical behavior. However, it only shapes the geometry of the update itself and does not explicitly control neighborhood worst-case loss or bias training toward flat regions, which are often linked to stronger generalization. To address this limitation, we introduce the sharpness-aware formulation of Muon. We first develop \textsc{MuSAM}, which incorporates Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM)-style perturbation into Muon's orthogonalized update and establishes a principled bridge between sharpness-aware training and matrix-aware optimization. We then show that this formulation faces an efficiency--stability tension: constructing the perturbation requires an extra gradient evaluation, while the perturbation itself is generated from a single minibatch gradient and is therefore noisy. To resolve this issue, we propose \textsc{MoMuSAM}, which turns Muon's momentum from an update variable into a sharpness-probing signal. By using normalized momentum as the perturbation direction, \textsc{MoMuSAM} removes the extra clean-gradient computation and replaces the single-batch perturbation proxy with a temporally aggregated one. We prove that both \textsc{MuSAM} and \textsc{MoMuSAM} attain the same asymptotic first-order stationarity order, and further show that \textsc{MoMuSAM} yields a more stable stochastic linearization than \textsc{MuSAM}. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that sharpness awareness consistently improves Muon.
  </details>

- **[SimplexLoRA: Dynamic Rank Updating via Alternating Minimization with Simplex Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37206)**  `arXiv:2610.37206`  
  _Igor Shalygin, Grigorii Davydenko, Andrey Veprikov, Aleksey Antonov, Nikolay Bushkov, Stanislav Moiseev, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The success of large language models in real-world applications relies on parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), which inserts compact, trainable adapters into selected layers. In this paper, we propose SimplexLoRA, a LoRA variant that adaptively adjusts adapter ranks during fine-tuning based on their learned importance. Unlike standard LoRA, which employs a fixed rank across all layers, SimplexLoRA optimizes per-adapter importance weights on the $n$-scaled simplex during a short warm-up and then freezes them, dynamically allocating capacity where it is most beneficial without increasing the overall parameter budget or inference cost. We evaluate SimplexLoRA across 12 language understanding, summarization, and reasoning tasks using DeBERTaV3, BART-large, Llama 3.1 8B, and Qwen3-8B. Our results show that SimplexLoRA consistently matches or outperforms standard LoRA and recent adaptive baselines, with the largest gains at low ranks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Action-Value Calibration via Continuation Sharing for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37195)**  `arXiv:2610.37195`  
  _Yingjian Chen, Lang Zhou, Zixin Zhao, Pingfan Li, Weishi Zheng, Zhilin Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuation. The resulting local credit remains sensitive to decisions made later in the trajectory. To address this problem, we propose ConCal (Continuation Calibration), a recursive continuation-value estimator that incorporates continuation evidence already present in a rollout batch. ConCal matches visits from the same task by their incoming state-action-state transitions, combines shared continuation evidence with each visit's own transition estimate, and propagates calibrated values backward to refine earlier action-value estimates. This reduces dependence on individual sampled continuations and mitigates local credit distortions without additional rollouts. Held-out return diagnostics show lower error in within-group value differences and higher agreement with reference action orderings than estimates based on individual continuations. With Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, ConCal improves success rates over GiGPO by 3.39 and 5.86 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MLE-World: Predicting Machine Learning Experiment Outcomes with a Learned World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37175)**  `arXiv:2610.37175`  
  _Brian Ko, Han Wang, Hejia Geng, Li Erran Li, Sherry Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI research agents can propose, execute, and improve programs that solve machine learning tasks. However, executing each candidate program requires training and evaluating ML models, limiting how many candidates an agent can explore at each step. We introduce MLE-World, a world model finetuned from Qwen3.5-4B that predicts a program's terminal output (error traces, training logs, and validation scores) from its code and data description without executing it, together with a confidence score estimating the prediction's correctness. Because a prediction costs one inference call rather than a training run, MLE-World makes test time scaling (TTS) affordable for these agents. We build five TTS policies, guided by MLE-World, that compare several candidates per step by their predicted outputs: Sequential revision, Best-of-N, Pairwise judging, Fusion, and a UCB style rule that also adds an exploration bonus from this confidence score. Since predictions can be wrong, we run a candidate for real only when its confidence score is low. On 17 MLE-bench Lite tasks, MLE-World matches the real terminal output on 77.9% of held out programs, versus 41.1% for much larger prompted models. MLE-World guided TTS achieves up to 22.1% higher observed mean performance than the agent without a world model, with less real execution. Project webpage:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Variational Optimal Control for Diffusion Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37146)**  `arXiv:2610.37146`  
  _Ruoyu Wang, Xinyi Ye, Yunshuo Shi, Liangyu Yuan, Beier Zhu, Chi Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a variational control framework that connects sampling-time guidance and reward-based model adaptation through a shared Jacobian pullback. Starting from terminal reward maximization with a path-space KL penalty, the Gibbs variational principle and Doob transform yield a correction to the generative dynamics. A local denoising approximation exposes the pullback that transports output preferences to the controlled variable. This construction gives Variational Optimal Guidance (VOG) in state space and extends to Distilled Variational Optimal Control (DVOC) by amortizing locally improved endpoints into model parameters. The resulting controls share a local variational rule: VOG acts through the denoising Jacobian, while DVOC realizes the parameter pullback by endpoint distillation. Under a linear MMSE model, VOG's exact correction lies in the data subspace. On ImageNet, VOG reduces DiT-XL/2 FID from 2.27 to 2.04 at 250 sampling steps and from 7.58 to 4.44 at 25 steps. After 20 updates on Z-Image-Turbo, DVOC improves HPSv3++ and four held-out evaluator scores over the untrained model, with the highest corresponding means among the trained methods compared. These results connect inference-time control and reward adaptation through a common geometric principle.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Parameter Protection: Fisher-Shaped EWC for Replay-Free Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37145)**  `arXiv:2610.37145`  
  _Liying Wang, Simone Magistri, Joost van de Weijer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In replay-free continual learning, Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC) uses Fisher information matrix (FIM) to protect acquired knowledge by penalizing changes to historically important parameters. However, a local importance estimate at the retained solution does not in general characterize sensitivity after finite parameter displacement. This motivates controlling Fisher sensitivity over a neighborhood of potential future updates. We propose Fisher-Shaped EWC (FS-EWC), which extends the role of FIM from parameter protection to solution formation for future retention. During task learning, FS-EWC minimizes the worst-case empirical FIM trace within a prescribed neighborhood, explicitly favoring solutions that are less sensitive to bounded future displacement. At task completion, trajectory-based model merging constructs the retained solution from multiple optimization states rather than a single terminal iterate, after which FIM is estimated for subsequent consolidation. Experiments across multiple benchmarks show that FS-EWC improves performance over EWC, while controlled studies indicate a more favorable stability--plasticity balance and greater tolerance to parameter drift. These results suggest that effective knowledge retention requires both forming solutions with future retention in mind and protecting acquired knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Generative Replay for Equilibrium Propagation: Toward Edge-Friendly Continual Learning in Hopfield Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37139)**  `arXiv:2610.37139`  
  _Victor Cook, Gilles Sassatelli, Thierry Gil, Mohamed Watfa, Marina Reyboz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equilibrium Propagation (EqProp) offers a biologically grounded and hardware-aligned alternative to backpropagation for training Hopfield Networks. However, its behavior in continual learning settings remains largely unexplored. This work confronts catastrophic forgetting in EqProp-trained Hopfield Networks from the inside out, eliminating the need for external data buffers or architectural overhead that standard Experience Replay \textbf{(ER)} and Generative Replay \textbf{(Gen-Replay)} inevitably require. We introduce an Energy-Based Generative Replay strategy \textbf{(EB-Gen)} which exploits the intrinsic generative dynamics of Hopfield Networks, allowing a single model to act as both classifier and replay samples generator. Samples capturing past task knowledge are generated by relaxing a frozen copy of the network, enabling rehearsal solely through internally generated pseudo-data. We evaluate the proposed method in the Class-Incremental setting, using the split versions of the MNIST and CIFAR10 benchmarks and compare it with FineTune, Experience Replay, Generative Replay, Sleep-Like Replay Consolidation (SRC) and Joint training. Our results show that EB-Gen significantly improves stability while preserving plasticity, achieving a 75\% and 64\% average accuracy on Split MNIST and Split CIFAR10 respectively. These findings show that Equilibrium Propagation can be adapted to alleviate catastrophic forgetting without forgoing the traits which made it compelling in the first place.
  </details>

- **[On the Sparsity of Direct Preference Optimization: Weight Disentanglement in the NTK Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37085)**  `arXiv:2610.37085`  
  _Kensuke Sasaki, Issei Sato_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) has been widely adopted for aligning language models with human preferences. Recent empirical work has revealed that DPO induces remarkably sparse parameter updates, yet no theoretical explanation for this phenomenon has been offered. We analyze DPO's optimization dynamics under a neural tangent kernel linearization and show that the logistic structure of the DPO loss gives rise to an implicit maximum-margin bias. Building on this observation, we introduce the concept of dual sparsity: the DPO update direction is a sparse linear combination (data-level sparsity, driven by support vectors) of sparse gradient feature vectors (parameter-level sparsity, arising from gradient cancellation between preferred and dispreferred responses). We formalize this structure in our main theorem, bound the number of support pairs by a margin-dependent spectral quantity, and present a framework relating dual sparsity to weight disentanglement---a condition favorable for model merging. Experiments on GPT-2 Small and Medium and on 7--9B instruction-tuned models (Llama-3-8B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9B) with the Anthropic HH-RLHF and UltraFeedback datasets confirm both sources of sparsity, and experiments on GPT-2 show that DPO reduces cross-task interference by up to $35{\times}$ compared to supervised fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Crop Yield Prediction Using Regression Models: A Comparative Evaluation of Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37022)**  `arXiv:2610.37022`  
  _Saptak Das, Arijit Sarkar, Soumik Neogi, Suborno Naskar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable crop-yield prediction can support agricultural planning and resource allocation. This study evaluates five regression models for predicting crop yield from environmental, crop-related, and agricultural management variables. The dataset initially contained 1,000,000 observations and ten variables, with 231 observations having negative target values removed during preprocessing. Categorical variables were represented using one-hot encoding, and Boolean variables were converted into binary values. The models considered were multiple linear regression (LR), random-forest regression (RF), decision-tree regression (DT), ridge regression, and lasso regression. Their performance was evaluated using 80:20 and 70:30 training-testing splits based on the coefficient of determination (R-squared), mean squared error (MSE), and root mean squared error (RMSE). Applying an 80:20 split, LR achieved a test R-squared of approximately 0.9130 and a test RMSE of 0.5008. Applying a 70:30 split, lasso regression yielded the highest test R-squared (approximately 0.9131) and the lowest test RMSE (approximately 0.5008), with performance almost identical to LR and ridge regression. DT also showed improved test performance under the 70:30 split, whereas RF exhibited a substantial gap between training and testing performance. These results indicate that the evaluated linear regression model provides competitive baselines for this dataset. Further experimentation, including validation testing, will help determine the models' performance and predictability.
  </details>

- **[Meta Models: Models that Model Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37001)**  `arXiv:2610.37001`  
  _Peihang Li, Ziming Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every neural-network training run produces not only a model, but also evidence about how models learn. Can these accumulated experiments enable us to predict and compare new training runs before executing them? We study meta-models: models that predict the learning behavior of other models. From this perspective, final-performance prediction, learning-curve prediction, and hyperparameter selection are different queries to the same training response function. We take a first, humble empirical step toward this goal: treating real language-model training records as native numeric data, we find that an off-the-shelf tabular in-context learner, TabPFN, can match or outperform a fine-tuned LLM meta-model in multiple evaluated settings. Whereas recent work fine-tunes LLMs on tokenized configurations at a cost of tens to hundreds of GPU-hours, TabPFN requires seconds of fit-and-predict time without task-specific weight updates. We argue that meta-modeling is better treated as its own numeric modality rather than as a text task for LLMs. Going one step further, augmenting TabPFN with Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) further improves accuracy on selected prediction tasks. Finally, we pre-registered a forecast of the Marin Hero Run, a 535B-parameter public training run, whose ground truth is revealed point by point as training progresses, putting meta-model predictions to the test against training outcomes not yet observed at the time of forecasting.
  </details>

- **[CaLLM: Continual Adaptation of LLMs via Non-Parametric Adapter Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36994)**  `arXiv:2610.36994`  
  _Bilge Celik, Anna Vettoruzzo, Joaquin Vanschoren_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual adaptation of large language models (LLMs) to evolving task distributions while mitigating catastrophic forgetting remains an open problem. Existing strategies often struggle to scale or lack a robust mechanism for zero-shot task-to-expert assignment. We introduce CaLLM, a framework that resolves these tensions by combining metric-based meta-learning with modular parameter isolation via lightweight adapters. First, CaLLM projects each input batch into a task-agnostic embedding space to find its nearest prototype. It then routes the batch to the specific adapter specialized for that data distribution. This formulation provides (i)~controllable cross-task interference by design, (ii)~constant computational complexity regardless of task count, (iii)~seamless integration of novel distributions through prototype expansion, and (iv)~regime agnosticism as the local routing decision enables a unified approach to both online and offline settings. Our experiments demonstrate that this routing mechanism discovers semantically coherent task groupings while facilitating constructive interference between similar tasks, and keeping overhead at a minimum. Across diverse and fine-grained continual learning benchmarks, CaLLM consistently outperforms traditional isolation and replay methods, establishing prototype-based modular routing as a principled and scalable approach to lifelong adaptation of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Advancement or Acceleration? How Data Curation Improves LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36983)**  `arXiv:2610.36983`  
  _Jiaxing Wang, Zicheng Zhang, Deping Xiang, Jin Xu, Yong Li, Chao Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While data curation is widely credited with improving large language model training, a basic question remains unresolved: does curation expand what a model can ultimately learn, or merely reach the same solution faster? In this paper, we formalize this distinction between advancement (improving the attainable performance) and acceleration (reaching the same performance with less data). Through an analytic characterization and real-world LLM experiments, we show that advancement requires a correctable train-target gap. When the baseline training and target objectives coincide, no curation policy improves the endpoint, although curation may still accelerate training. This reframes advancement as a property of the curation task rather than the curation method. Under a common gap-centric view of data mixture and selection, we operationalize this condition as the $\Delta$-test, an observable discrepancy between the uncurated baseline's training and target losses that is computable prior to expensive curation. Across extensive experiments, an appreciably nonzero $\Delta$ predicts persistent gains, whereas gains near $\Delta=0$ tend to diminish as the training budget grows. Beyond individual methods, our findings recenter data curation on the task itself and point to target-set construction as a promising direction for shaping what curation can achieve.
  </details>

- **[GNN-Guided Network Reduction for Centralized Urban Traffic Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36981)**  `arXiv:2610.36981`  
  _Nikolai Antonov, Lukáš Chrpa, Andrii Nyporko, Jan Hůla_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We consider the centralized traffic routing (CTR) problem, where a central planner assigns routes to origin-destination (OD) vehicle flows to minimize congestion-dependent segment cost. Prior work formulates CTR efficiently as a mixed-integer linear program (MILP), but scaling this formulation to the full road network is computationally prohibitive within an operational time budget. Bounded Routes and Smart Routes speed up optimization using precomputed reduced networks; however, these methods do not account for vehicle information within each OD flow. Motivated by this gap, we propose a learning-based urban network reduction approach. A dedicated graph neural network (GNN) first scores road segments for each OD flow using the road network and vehicle-flow information. Highly ranked segments are then incorporated into complete OD routes, and the original segment-flow MILP is solved on the resulting reduced road network. The GNN thus guides the reduction of the road network, while the solver enforces feasibility and determines the routing. Experiments on four real urban networks -- Prague-Dejvice, London, New York, and Sydney -- show that the proposed approach achieves the smallest relative objective difference to the $30$-second full-graph reference in every city under both traffic regimes. Averaged across cities, this difference is more than six times lower than that of the best competing method. Moreover, traffic simulation results show that the optimization gains of the proposed method lead to more efficient routing, with the highest aggregate arrival rate and a competitive trip duration per arrival percentage point.
  </details>

- **[On the VC Dimension of Autoregressive Linear Threshold Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36970)**  `arXiv:2610.36970`  
  _Ilan Doron-Arad, Idan Mehalel, Elchanan Mossel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear threshold functions (LTFs) are a canonical class of Boolean predictors. In the autoregressive setting, the same LTF reads the most recent $d$ bits, generates the next bit, and returns the bit produced after $T$ steps. While the one-step class has VC dimension $d$, for general $T$ the counting upper bound is $O(d^2)$. We prove that the VC dimension is $\Theta(d^2)$ for every $T\ge d^2$, resolving the quadratic gap for deterministic autoregressive LTFs. We then adapt the same technique to show an exponential separation between deterministic and stochastic autoregressive models: replacing the sign of LTFs by logistic sampling can increase the VC dimension of the final majority prediction class from quadratic to exponential in $d$.
  </details>

- **[Live-OPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Proactive Streaming Large Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36959)**  `arXiv:2610.36959`  
  _Kaixuan Du, Xin Wan, Dai Guan, Ming Chen, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming multimodal assistants must make response decisions while events are still unfolding. They need to recognize when the available evidence warrants a response, produce an appropriate continuation, and remain silent otherwise. Supervised fine-tuning learns from recorded assistant turns, but does not directly train on the response prefixes produced by the evolving policy itself. We introduce Live-OPSD, an on-policy self-distillation method that uses privileged hints available only during training to supervise the policy on its own outputs. The policy first generates a continuation from the streaming context alone. The same model then re-evaluates that continuation with a hint describing the relevant evidence, and the resulting distributions guide the unhinted policy. On Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B, Live-OPSD attains an average score of 59.64 on LiveProBench, 5.26 points above the strongest continued-SFT baseline, and stays competitive on OVO-Bench (65.84). These results indicate that guiding the policy on its own outputs with privileged hints improves both when and how a streaming assistant responds.
  </details>

- **[DCKV: Learned Transform Coding of the Prompt KV Cache in Hybrid Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36950)**  `arXiv:2610.36950`  
  _Egor Maximov, Aleksei Merkelov, Vasiliy Alekseev, Ivan Krivorotov, Valentin Beliaev, Nikita Arsenin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid language models keep a key-value (KV) cache only in their few full-attention layers, but this cache is still the part of a prompt's state that grows with the prompt and that must be stored or moved to reuse it. We present DCKV, a differentiable codec for the KV cache and the first KV-cache codec that is trained end to end. Its transform, its quantizer and its entropy models, which are conditioned on the prompt tokens and on already decoded neighbouring positions, are optimized together against the frozen language model, both for the code length that the entropy models assign and for the change of the model's output. On Qwen3.5-2B, DCKV stores a compressed token in more than 55 times fewer bytes than bf16 at a loss of at most 1.8 points on three long-context benchmarks, and in more than 100 times fewer bytes at a loss of at most 3.6 points.
  </details>

- **[TDLM: Adaptive Temporal Integration for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36940)**  `arXiv:2610.36940`  
  _Bohang Sun, Max Zhu, Francesco Caso, Christopher Irwin, Jialin Yu, Pietro Liò_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked diffusion language models repeatedly compute representations for unresolved tokens, but conventional denoisers do not explicitly retain these representations for reuse across steps. We introduce Temporal Self-Conditioning Diffusion Language Model (TDLM), which lets each unresolved position attend to its own history of hidden representations within a frozen denoiser. This gives later predictions access to computation already performed. Training uses self-generated trajectories and a simple cross-entropy objective for tokens revealed between consecutively sampled decoding steps, while causal temporal attention permits parallel evaluation during training. On LLaDA, the temporal modules add only approximately 0.84% trainable parameters. Across LLaDA and Dream, the main comparison shows up to 7.15$\times$ faster generation than the corresponding original diffusion language model, with competitive answer quality on the evaluated tasks. These results motivate temporal memory as a practical way to make more effective use of repeated denoising computation.
  </details>

- **[Gaussian Process Bandits for Constraint Black Box Lever Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36935)**  `arXiv:2610.36935`  
  _Bram van den Akker, Hidde Fokkema, Anastasiia Kornilova_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing discrete allocation weights across competing segments of a shared budget is a fundamental challenge in resource-constrained, sequential decision-making environments. In practice, this optimization is often hindered by delayed, noisy, and aggregate feedback, alongside strict operational constraints on how frequently weights can be adjusted. Because of the resulting combinatorial action space and extreme data sparsity, classical multi-armed bandit algorithms that evaluate arms independently are rendered highly inefficient. To overcome these limitations, we propose a segmentation-agnostic approach utilizing Gaussian Process Upper Confidence Bound (GP-UCB) algorithms. By placing a Gaussian Process prior on the reward function, our method effectively shares information across neighboring configurations, drastically reducing the necessary exploration horizon. In this paper, we bridge the gap between theoretical bandit research and production-scale deployment. We first introduce a high-fidelity synthetic simulator to evaluate various kernel configurations under stationary, drifting, and reactive environments, identifying a composite Matérn + Linear kernel as the most robust choice for overcoming local noise and global shifts. Finally, we validate our findings through a large-scale Randomized Controlled Trial (RCT) in a live production environment. Our empirical results demonstrate that the GP-UCB approach delivers a statistically significant efficiency lift compared to a standard $\varepsilon$-greedy baseline, offering a highly practical solution for real-world black-box lever optimization.
  </details>

- **[Provenance Signatures for Low Rank Adaptations of Image Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36933)**  `arXiv:2610.36933`  
  _Tobias Cheung, Mohan Dantam, Marc Juarez, Rik Sarkar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have revolutionized the generation of realistic artificial images. Efficient techniques such as Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA) can modify these models for particular use cases, making them more diverse and widely usable. At the same time, open base models may be adapted to deliver biased, harmful or illegal images in an online service. In this paper, we develop a protocol to detect when a service may be using an adaptation of an otherwise standard model. We describe the precise setup and threat model for this provenance question and define two different problems arising due the presence of multiple model families and multiple adaptations. In contrast to previous works that have considered model identification within lists of known candidate models, we consider the realistic open world scenario where the set of possible adaptations and alternative models are unknown. Using a hypothesis testing approach, our protocols can distinguish between the base model, a LoRA adaptation and other completely different families of models. For this test, we develop a black box signature of the models based only on intrinsic dimension of sets of images generated by the models.
  </details>

- **[Hyperparameter and Neural Architecture Search via Evolutionary Optimization for Reinforcement Learning: HPNAS-RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36882)**  `arXiv:2610.36882`  
  _Nihal Acharya Adde, Hanno Gottschalk_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this study, we propose a dual-branch evolutionary framework that simultaneously performs Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) for Reinforcement Learning (RL) in Autonomous Driving (AD) tasks. While NAS has shown strong potential in automating architecture design for Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), its application to RL remains limited, especially in scenarios that require joint optimization of architectural and training dynamics. Building on our prior work on Evolutionary Multi-Objective NAS (EMNAS), the proposed framework evolves discrete network architectures and continuous hyperparameters in parallel under a unified multi-objective search strategy. In this setup, NAS targets the convolutional layers of the RL policy network - responsible for visual feature extraction, while HPO focuses on optimizing critical RL hyperparameters. To accelerate convergence and improve generalization, we incorporate parallel evaluation and a teacher-student policy transfer mechanism. Experiments in a high-fidelity driving simulator show that the joint NAS-HPO framework consistently outperforms standalone NAS, yielding policies with higher rewards, fewer parameters, and improved stability. These results highlight the benefits of jointly optimizing model structure and training dynamics for scalable and effective RL agent design.
  </details>

- **[Tail Cues, Principal Corrections: Plug-and-Play Rectification for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36870)**  `arXiv:2610.36870`  
  _Yingkai Yang, Chaoqi Chen, Hui Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation (OSTTA) operates on non-stationary target streams where covariate and semantic shifts coexist. Existing TTA methods often obtain adaptation signals by updating model parameters or maintaining mutable memories, which can be costly and vulnerable to unknown samples. We present TALOR, a lightweight, backward-free rectification module that uses the source linear head as a geometric anchor. The head-induced basis decomposes each normalized feature into high-gain principal coordinates and low-gain tail coordinates. TALOR estimates a soft-routed affine correction in the principal subspace, combining a regime-level principal bias with a tail-conditioned slope that captures sample-level tail-to-principal coupling. Using weighted ridge regression, it subtracts this drift estimate from the principal coordinates, reconstructs the feature and feeds it into the source head. On the ImageNet-C benchmark with six csOOD datasets, TALOR outperforms the next-best UniEnt by 2.2% H-score while running 2.5x faster and using only 12% of its GPU memory; as a post-correction plug-in, it further boosts COME to 68.4% H-score with only 2% additional memory overhead.
  </details>

- **[When All Rollouts Fail: Policy-Compatible Joint Decoding for Hard-Problem Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36849)**  `arXiv:2610.36849`  
  _Zehao Wang, Hanzhu Chen, Runquan Gui, Zhe Wang, Lin Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) derives its learning signal from binary rewards via on-policy exploration and becomes a powerful paradigm for improving the reasoning of large language models. Yet it faces a learning cliff on problems beyond model's current ability: when every rollout fails, the samples reveal nothing about a correct solution, and group-relative estimators (e.g., GRPO, RLOO) collapse the update to zero advantage. Expert trajectories offer a remedy, but they are incompatible with the current policy and often lie in the student's low-probability regions, so the surviving updates demand large parameter shifts and decay into surface-level imitation rather than transferable reasoning. We argue that a useful learning signal on hard problems must be both verifiably correct and sufficiently compatible with the current policy. We propose Policy-Compatible Reinforcement Learning (PICO), which performs distribution-constrained joint decoding: expert data guides decoding at the token level while preserving the student's relative token probabilities, and a dynamic top-k mechanism confines the sampling distribution under a finite divergence budget, yielding trajectories that are verified and policy-compatible by construction. These trajectories are optimized jointly with on-policy rollouts under a mixed-behavior objective that mitigates their distribution mismatch via importance sampling. Experiments across reasoning diverse datasets and agentic benchmarks demonstrate that PICO consistently outperforms standard RLVR and prior off-policy baselines.
  </details>

- **[SetGRPO: Calibrating Grader-Induced Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36807)**  `arXiv:2610.36807`  
  _Jiale Huang, Zixu Li, Zhiwei Chen, Zhiheng Fu, Jinhe Bi, Yupeng Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based reinforcement learning uses fine-grained grader feedback to train large language models on open-ended tasks, but grader randomness can alter GRPO updates in ways that score discrepancies alone cannot characterize. Because GRPO jointly normalizes rewards within each response group and combines the resulting training coefficients with response-level gradients, a scoring discrepancy can shift the coefficients of the entire group and induce policy-dependent update changes. Selective execution further affects the direction-reversal risk of retained updates. To address this, we propose Set-Valued Group Relative Policy Optimization (SetGRPO). It comprises three modules: (a) Joint Reward Set Calibration constructs a joint uncertainty set for the training coefficients; (b) Geometry-Guided Safe Recovery maps candidate coefficients into the current policy's local update geometry and determines the largest safe recovery ratio for each response group; and (c) Selective Risk-Gated Optimization selects a global threshold through independent risk calibration to control the conditional direction-reversal risk of accepted updates while preserving the original GRPO objective. Under exchangeability, SetGRPO provides finite-sample marginal coverage for the joint coefficient set and simultaneous risk control across candidate thresholds. On ten open-ended benchmarks spanning three domains, SetGRPO achieves the highest macro average among all compared training methods on both Qwen3.5 and Llama3.2 model families.
  </details>

- **[Does LLM Pre-Training Typically Occur at the Edge of Stability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36778)**  `arXiv:2610.36778`  
  _Yuhang Cai, Haodong Wen, Haofeng Huang, Deyi Liu, Yiyuan Ma, Kaifeng Lyu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quadratic approximations provide a common lens for studying neural network optimization, but recent evidence challenges their predictive validity. In full-batch gradient descent with learning rate (LR) $\eta$, Cohen et al. (2021) observed the Edge of Stability (EoS), where the largest Hessian eigenvalue concentrates near $2/\eta$, seemingly at odds with classical stability conditions predicted by quadratic approximations. In this work, we revisit the fidelity of quadratic approximations as a model of neural network training dynamics, with a particular focus on their failure modes in LLM training. We first highlight a distinct failure mode of the quadratic approximation, where persistent negative curvature causes the quadratic dynamics to diverge regardless of the LR, even though the real dynamics remain stable. We term this phenomenon the Edge of Convexity (EoC). After removing the effect of this LR-independent instability, we then propose a generalized definition of Edge of Stability (EoS) for stochastic training with adaptive optimizers, based on whether increasing the LR induces significant divergence in the quadratic dynamics while leaving the real dynamics largely unchanged. Across different LLM pretraining runs with various model sizes up to 787M, we find that (1) EoC is always observed across LLM pretraining, and (2) EoS is also prevalent but not universal: it disappears when the LR becomes sufficiently small (e.g., after decay) or when the batch size falls below a critical threshold that has a positive correlation with the critical batch size. Together, these findings characterize when and how quadratic approximations fail and provide a foundation for future work on understanding training dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Robust LLM Data Selection via Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36755)**  `arXiv:2610.36755`  
  _Jia Gu, Liang Pang, Huawei Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient-based data attribution has become an important tool for high-quality data selection in the instruction fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs), as it estimates the contribution of training examples through their influence on model optimization. However, we find that existing global gradient attribution methods suffer from surface-form bias: minor changes to semantically irrelevant details, such as wording or formatting, can produce highly inconsistent attribution scores even when the underlying content remains unchanged. To address this issue, we propose Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution (CIGA), a robust framework for instruction data selection. CIGA first identifies layers whose gradients are both influential for attribution and stable under semantics-preserving surface-form perturbations, thereby reducing formatting noise in gradient features. It then dynamically fuses these attribution signals with semantic representations of the input, combining optimization-level influence with content-level information. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show that CIGA produces more stable rankings under surface-form perturbations while preserving downstream capabilities and improving average performance. CIGA also reduces attribution cost, offering a robust and efficient approach to data selection for LLM instruction tuning.
  </details>

- **[Nonlinear Direct Feedback Alignment for Scalable Backpropagation-Free Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36718)**  `arXiv:2610.36718`  
  _Wu Lin, Yan Fan, Chenxiang Ma, Yinglan Feng, Jia Wang, Ka-Chun Wong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Backpropagation (BP) remains the predominant algorithm for training deep neural networks, but it is biologically implausible and suffers from the backward locking problem, constraining parallelization across layers. Direct Feedback Alignment~(DFA), which propagates the global output error directly to each hidden layer via fixed feedback matrices, has emerged as a promising alternative for addressing these limitations. However, a significant performance gap persists between DFA and BP, which we attribute to the limited ability of fixed feedback matrices to capture the nonlinear transformation from hidden representations to the network output. To address this limitation, we propose Nonlinear Direct Feedback Alignment (NDFA), which constructs each hidden-layer feedback using a lightweight nonlinear surrogate subnetwork. To enhance the effectiveness of the feedback throughout training, we further propose a learning strategy to update the surrogate subnetwork, thereby better approximating the underlying nonlinear transformation. Extensive experiments across residual networks and vision transformers on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, SVHN, and ImageNet datasets demonstrate that NDFA consistently outperforms existing DFA-based methods and substantially narrows the performance gap to BP, while maintaining efficient parallel training. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RankFormer: Rethinking Time Series Transformers through Representation Capacity Analysis and Progressive Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36703)**  `arXiv:2610.36703`  
  _Peiwang Tang, Cheng HE, Xijie Liang, Defu Lian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series forecasting plays a critical role in Web and cyber-physical systems, where large-scale services and IoT infrastructures generate complex temporal data. Transformer-based models have achieved strong empirical performance in this domain, yet the principles governing the expressive capacity of their learned representations remain insufficiently understood. Moreover, many existing models rely on simplified decoding strategies, including channel-independent modeling and direct projection heads, which limit their ability to capture complex multivariate dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose RankFormer, a novel encoder-decoder architecture for multivariate time series forecasting. We establish a rank-based analysis framework to investigate how tokenization, embedding, and attention designs affect the representation capacity of time series Transformers. Our analysis reveals that patchification increases the potential representation capacity by aggregating multiple temporal observations into each token, while subsequent encoding operations determine how much of this capacity is realized. Guided by these insights, we develop a rank-enhancing encoder with Hourglass Attention to improve rank stability during information interaction. Furthermore, we introduce the Interactive Progressive Decoder (IPD), which improves future trajectory modeling through global-guided cross-channel interaction and progressive refinement. Extensive experiments on real-world long-term forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that RankFormer achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods. Code repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[HeteGCD: Statistics-Aware Dual-Prototype Learning for Generalized Category Discovery on Heterophilous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36696)**  `arXiv:2610.36696`  
  _Xuanzhi Xi, Tongze Zhang, Zhili Qin, Yulin Tao, Hongliang Wang, Zhong Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heterophilous graphs are prevalent in real-world applications, where connected nodes often exhibit dissimilar attributes or belong to different classes, posing significant challenges to conventional graph learning methods based on homophily. This limitation becomes particularly pronounced in generalized category discovery (GCD), which requires models to recognize known classes and discover novel ones using labels from known classes only. However, existing graph GCD methods typically rely on aforementioned homophily-based inductive biases, which are ill-suited to heterophilous graphs. Meanwhile, although current heterophilous graph learning methods exploit diverse neighborhood signals, their selection and fusion are often guided by supervision across all classes, including novel classes whose labels are unavailable in GCD. These limitations raise a key question: how can heterogeneous neighborhood information be effectively leveraged for category discovery without any novel-class supervision? To answer this question, in this study we propose HeteGCD, a new statistics-aware dual-prototype learning perspective for generalized category discovery on heterophilous graphs. The key idea is to examine both statistics of local attribute differences and neighborhood class distribution to extract discriminative information in feature and probability spaces simultaneously. Specifically, a gated dual-stream encoder integrates both node attributes and local attribute-difference statistics to learn informative node representations. Afterwards, a dual-prototype classifier combines feature-based similarity with distribution-based consistency to support category discovery. Experiments on five heterophilous graphs show that HeteGCD achieves competitive performance against 24 baseline combinations.
  </details>

- **[TACO-Graph: Automated Construction of Text-Atributed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36650)**  `arXiv:2610.36650`  
  _Amiri Hayes, Kristina Wicke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph-structured representations of text data have become foundational across machine learning, yet it remains unclear how to systematically construct a graph from a given dataset for a target task. Choices about which entities become nodes, which relationships become edges, and what text each node contains can substantially affect downstream performance. In practice, finding optimal constructions often requires expert knowledge or manual trial and error. We introduce TACO-Graph, a decision tree framework for learning which graph construction choices are most effective for a given dataset and task. Specifically, we focus on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) constructed from multi-attribute tabular data. TACO-Graph enumerates valid TAG representations, scores each using fast proxy evaluation on small representative subsets, and trains an interpretable decision tree on those scores. Across five datasets spanning citation networks, product reviews, and bibliographic relationships, our decision trees predict construction quality on separately drawn evaluation samples with mean Spearman $\rho$ above $0.9$ for node classification and above $0.8$ for GraphRAG retrieval. Our trees systematically identify which constructions perform better across both GNN and GraphRAG tasks, and the full pipeline is released as an open-source library to support evaluation on new tasks and datasets atthis https URL. TACO-Graph provides a systematic and interpretable guide for determining how to convert tabular data to text-attributed graphs for downstream tasks.
  </details>

- **[When Is a Cheap Fairness Edit Safe? Screening Post-Hoc Edits in Two-Sided Matching Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36649)**  `arXiv:2610.36649`  
  _Moirangthem Tiken Singha, Rabinder Kumar Prasada_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two-sided matching systems, such as admissions pipelines, allocate opportunities jointly. Reducing a protected at tribute's influence usually requires encoder retraining. We study a closed-form linear projector that does this at inference time. For markets that stay vertically ordered before and after the edit, we bound the mean welfare change by the edit-induced appeal shift and an explicit remainder. A probabilistic refinement replaces worst-case constants with a data-dependent deviation term. A pre-deployment screen combines a verticality test with a welfare sweep and returns PASS, KILL, or OUT-OF-SCOPE. An exact tier requires every opportunity to rank applicants by appeal, and a graded tier admits near-vertical markets. On 30 held-out synthetic markets, the screen agrees with every pre-specified regime verdict, with no observed false PASS. On 60 exactly vertical markets, no bound is violated, and the probabilistic bound is informative in 69% to 87% of configurations. On 2700 configurations from three realistic markets, which do not reach the exact tier, no plug-in bound expression is violated. A closed-form factorization stays within graded scope in 90% to 100% of instances, whereas two-tower and graph neural network encoders fall outside it in every instance. Architecture choice thus appears to affect whether a post-hoc edit is admissible.
  </details>

- **[When Does Perturbation Consistency Add Information Beyond Softmax Confidence? Theory and Evidence for the Decision Consistency Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36640)**  `arXiv:2610.36640`  
  _Amit Kalitaa, Moirangthem Tiken Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Perturbation consistency scores judge whether a prediction survives small input changes. The Decision Consistency Framework (DCF) scores each image by label stability (LS) and confidence deviation (CD) under six mild operators, combines them into a consistency score (CS), and summarises a model by the gap between accuracy and mean consistency (CAG). We ask when these scores carry information that the clean softmax output lacks. Across 201 evaluations with three seeds per configuration, CS detected misclassifications on clean data less well than the maximum softmax probability (MSP) in every model family. For frozen CNNs, the deficit was 0.057 in area under the ROC curve (AUROC; 95% CI 0.043 to 0.073), at 8 to 15 times the compute. A label flip requires the logit margin to be at most the square root of two times the logit displacement, and at a fixed margin, the displacement predicted errors at AUROC 0.515. The same inequality gives a label-free split-conformal no-flip certificate, never violated in 84 runs. Consistency did add information where confidence is misled: under noise corruptions of CIFAR-10-C, it beat the margin, and under projected gradient attacks, it separated clean from attacked inputs with AUROC 0.83 against 0.37 for MSP at a budget of 2/255, as pre-specified. The gain under noise did not transfer to unseen shift families. The large negative CAG on OCTMNIST persists under full fine-tuning and in-domain training and is partly a class-prior effect: correcting the prior recovers about 40% of the gap.
  </details>

- **[The Batch Size Can Be One: Test-Time Adaptation from Classifier Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36628)**  `arXiv:2610.36628`  
  _Jaeho Hwang, Yujin Oh, Juhyeon Shin, Juhye Lee, Sungroh Yoon, Uiwon Hwang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time adaptation updates a deployed model on an unlabeled test stream. In practice this stream often arrives one sample at a time, with the classes in no particular order. A single sample then provides limited information for estimating distribution-level statistics, and selecting samples by confidence can admit predictions that are wrong yet confident. A corruption, however, shifts the features while leaving the decision regions of the frozen classifier unchanged. We therefore propose Origin-recentered Test-Time Adaptation (OriTTA), which constructs fixed geometric targets from the source model and adapts selected features toward them. Seen from the origin of the feature space, the classes lean toward a common direction. We choose a new origin to bring class scores closer together while keeping it near the mean feature of one synthetic prototype per class. From this origin, prototype directions spread more evenly, and each class obtains an axis and an ideal point before any test data arrives. During adaptation, OriTTA aligns selected features with their predicted class axis and penalizes excessive radius, keeps one input per predicted class in memory to limit feature changes for classes absent from the stream, and resets the model when re-classifying this memory reveals collapse. Without tuning for each setting, OriTTA achieves the highest mean accuracy at batch size 1 on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C and ImageNet-C in all sixteen combinations of four backbones, two protocols and two stream orders. On ImageNet-C fog, recent methods collapse in up to two thirds of one hundred runs, while OriTTA never does.
  </details>

- **[Dream-COP: Guiding Algorithm Discovery for Combinatorial Optimization Problems in an Experience World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36618)**  `arXiv:2610.36618`  
  _Kuan Xu, Jianming Wang, Ni Zhang, Yaoxin Wu, Yong Wang, Zhiguang Cao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) enable algorithm discovery for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs) through iterative program generation and evaluation. A central challenge is how to organize search to discover better algorithms within a limited budget. Our key insight is that past search trajectories and their recorded outcomes form an experience world, which can be used to facilitate the COP solving through replay. We introduce Dream-COP, in which a \emph{policy agent} learns executable policies through \emph{dreaming} in this experience world to guide a \emph{discovery agent} in generating and refining COP programs. Specifically, policy replay provides feedback on the quality of the programs revealed and the search steps used, to guide policy selection more effectively. Across five COPs, \textsc{Dream} discovers better algorithms than fixed exploration in both heuristic component design and complete solver generation. Particularly, it matches the optimal values on KP and reduces the gap on OBPP from 2.112\% to 1.199\% while using 42.5\% fewer search requests. Moreover, the learned exploration policies transfer effectively across different COPs with the assitance of the experience world.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Quantization Training with Stochastic Rounding via Structured Subspace Randomness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36587)**  `arXiv:2610.36587`  
  _Zeyu Wang, Yiheng Duan, Tao Chen, Donghyun Koo, Sihan Liu, Wenbo Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-precision training often relies on stochastic rounding (SR) to keep quantized gradients unbiased and prevent small updates from rounding to zero. Standard SR draws an independent random threshold for every gradient element. However, accelerators generate random numbers on vector units whose throughput lags far behind their matrix units. Drawing one threshold per element saturates the vector unit and can make INT8 training with SR slower than BF16 training. Sharing random thresholds across tensor elements removes this cost but forces those elements to round in the same direction and shifts their rounding errors by a common offset.
