We find that this offset degrades training primarily through the activation gradient, which propagates the error backward into earlier layers. Because earlier layers cannot cancel an incoming offset using their own local rounding errors, the offset must be removed at the layer where it originates. We propose Structured Subspace Stochastic Rounding (S3R), which quantizes each upstream gradient once for both the activation and weight gradient matrix multiplications and shares a single threshold per token across the hidden dimension. Its error correction, S3R-EC, subtracts the structured mean rounding error from the activation and weight gradients while leaving the optimizer unchanged. In LLM pre-training experiments, uncorrected threshold sharing degrades convergence, whereas S3R-EC eliminates this degradation and matches the validation loss of independent SR. Furthermore, S3R-EC accelerates full INT8 training steps of dense LLMs on TPU v5p by 1.24x to 1.32x over BF16 and by 1.11x over independent SR.
  </details>

- **[Less is more: learning what to profile for GPU kernel optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36576)**  `arXiv:2610.36576`  
  _Yihan Wang, Peiyu Liu, Runyu Chen, Wei Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have recently shown strong potential for automatically optimizing GPU kernels, yet their effectiveness depends critically on the hardware information available to the kernel optimization model. Existing systems typically rely on predefined profiling strategies, leaving what to profile largely outside the optimization process. We show that effective profiling is not simply about collecting more information: increasing the number of profiling metrics does not monotonically improve kernel optimization, while the most useful profiling information varies across kernels. Our theoretical analysis further shows that, under such heterogeneous profiling utility, kernel-conditioned selection can outperform any single fixed profiling configuration. Motivated by this observation, we propose AutoProfiler, a feedback-driven framework for learning a deterministic profiling tree from optimization feedback. The tree is iteratively refined through REWRITE and SPLIT operations, with an LLM generating candidate modifications and empirical evaluation determining their acceptance. Once constructed, the tree quickly gathers the required profiling information for any kernel, allowing the LLM rewriter to be invoked only once. Experiments on KernelBench show that our approach improves kernel optimization over fixed profiling configurations under constrained profiling budgets. These results suggest that hardware profiling should evolve from static metric collection toward profiling reasoning, making profiling itself a new dimension for improving kernel optimization. The codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Points and Patches: A Pre-trained Hybrid Attention Transformer for Time Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36572)**  `arXiv:2610.36572`  
  _Peiwang Tang, Cheng HE, Yupeng Ye, Weitai Zhang, Qinghua Sun, Defu Lian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series data generated by Web services and connected systems support applications such as user activity analysis, service monitoring, and resource management. However, learning transferable representations from heterogeneous time series remains challenging due to diverse temporal patterns, variable sequence lengths, and limited high-quality annotations. Despite the success of pre-training in natural language processing and computer vision, effective time-series pre-training requires strategies tailored to temporal data.We propose TimeHAT (Hybrid Attention Transformer), a general-purpose pre-training framework for time-series analysis that addresses two complementary questions: what to learn and how to learn. For what to learn, we introduce a Unified Point-Patch Masking Strategy (UPPMS) that integrates point-level and patch-level supervision within a unified framework for contextual reconstruction and causal extrapolation, enabling the model to capture both fine-grained temporal variations and broader temporal patterns. For how to learn, we develop a Hybrid Attention Encoder (HAE) that combines prefix-constrained bidirectional attention in initial layers with directional hybrid attention in deeper layers, aiming to preserve effective contextual interactions while mitigating rank degeneration.We evaluate TimeHAT across real-world datasets and four downstream tasks: classification, long-term forecasting, imputation, and anomaly detection. Experiments cover fine-tuning, few-shot, and zero-shot transfer settings, demonstrating competitive performance across diverse time-series analysis tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRISM: Pathology Reliability In Scarce-label Medicine. A Tile-level Benchmark of Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36517)**  `arXiv:2610.36517`  
  _Alicem Koyun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathology foundation models are typically benchmarked on classification performance at full label availability and on in-distribution test sets, while calibration, behavior at low label budgets, and robustness under cross-institutional shift are seldom evaluated jointly. We introduce PRISM, a tile-level benchmark that measures the reliability of pathology foundation models along four axes: model choice, label fraction, calibration, and out-of-distribution transfer. PRISM evaluates eight foundation models (CLIP, PLIP, CONCH, UNI, Virchow2, GigaPath, H-Optimus-0, Midnight) on six tile-level datasets at six label fractions (1% to 100%) with three seeds (864 linear probe runs), extended with a five-hospital Camelyon17 study of 20 directed transfer pairs. Four phenomena emerge. (i) Under full supervision, AUROC rank predicts post-temperature calibration rank consistently across datasets (Spearman rho = 0.52, I^2 = 0%), whereas at 1% labels the relationship becomes dataset-specific (rho = -0.24, I^2 = 67%). (ii) On harder datasets the linear probe collapses to a single predicted class below a dataset-specific label fraction. (iii) Whether additional source-domain labels help or harm target calibration tracks how well the probe transfers: below a transfer AUROC of roughly 0.85, more labels worsen target ECE in 86-88% of combinations. (iv) Temperature scaling fails to reduce ECE in 49 of 288 cells, miscalibration is predominantly under-confidence, and a source-fitted temperature is harmful under failing transfer. PRISM is released as a pip-installable package with reference results for all 288 cells. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36477)**  `arXiv:2610.36477`  
  _Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.
  </details>

- **[CoHARL: Co-Evolving Agent Policies and Executable Harnesses with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36469)**  `arXiv:2610.36469`  
  _Jianwen Chen, Peng Xia, Kaide Zeng, Siwei Han, Zhen-Yu Zhang, Charles Fleming, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through executable harnesses that govern tool use and feedback. Optimizing a harness for a fixed policy overlooks how its behavior changes during learning, while training a policy under a fixed harness leaves the interface that generates its training experience unchanged. This motivates adapting the harness throughout policy training. We introduce CoHARL, a population-based reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a shared policy and a population of executable harnesses. The policy learns from rollouts across the population, and a proposer uses its execution traces to revise harness code during training. CoHARL achieves a 27.6% mean resolved rate on SWE-rebench, improving over harness-only search by 6.4 percentage points. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show gains in software engineering and terminal-based tasks. Under matched training budgets, CoHARL also outperforms searching for a harness once before RL and alternating policy updates with revisions to a single active harness.
  </details>

- **[Simulated petrological labels enable probabilistic mineralogy prediction from whole-rock geochemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36429)**  `arXiv:2610.36429`  
  _Matthew J. Liu, Aaron D. Kaplan, Michael L. Whittaker, Kristin A. Persson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modal mineralogy, the mineral identities and abundances in rocks, guides geological studies and industrial resource extraction. While measuring modal mineralogy is time-intensive, whole-rock chemical measurements furnish elemental concentrations at higher throughput. Predicting mineralogy from elemental data, however, poses a one-to-many inverse problem, and labeled mineralogical datasets are too sparse to train on at scale. We address both challenges by simulating petrological labels that express multiple formation conditions per composition and at a scale unattainable by measurements. Nearly one million simulated labels supervise the middle component of a three-part model: a transformer encoder self-supervised on roughly 788,000 whole-rock compositions, the simulation-trained heads that predict distributions over mineral assemblages, and a recalibration layer that aligns the predictions with measured mineralogy. Zero-shot on 640 volcanic rocks, the model discriminates the presence of most mineral categories and predicts which rocks are richer in each mineral. However, the predicted abundances are systematically offset from measured values. Recalibration fit on as few as 25 labeled rocks recovers 76 percent of the likelihood improvement from fitting on the full 368-rock fitting set, while abundance error responds more slowly (11.0% zero-shot and 9.5% with the full fitting set). This work demonstrates how a machine learning model in the geochemical sciences can both leverage an imperfect simulator and mitigate its systematic biases.
  </details>

- **[Property-Based Testing for Robust Softmax and Cross-Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36427)**  `arXiv:2610.36427`  
  _Muhammad Maaz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A recent paper of Barron et. al. (2026) identified two numerical issues with the softmax and cross-entropy functions in virtually all standard machine learning libraries (e.g., PyTorch and JAX). We show that these issues can be recovered through simple property-based tests in Hypothesis. However, our tests identify an additional edge case where their implementation fails. We then propose an even more robust softmax implementation which passes these tests and also supports use in causal attention masking. We compare our implementation with theirs and discuss some of the differences. Overall, this work shows that property-based testing can be a powerful tool for discovering and fixing numerical issues in machine learning libraries. Our implementations and property-based tests can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Repulsive Population Policy Gradients for Behaviorally Diverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36425)**  `arXiv:2610.36425`  
  _Saeed Hedayatian, Harsh Shah, Celine Mneimneh, Stefanos Nikolaidis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The goal of Quality-Diversity Reinforcement Learning (QD-RL) is to find a population of high-performing policies that solve a decision-making problem in diverse ways. Despite recent progress, QD-RL methods predominantly rely on evolutionary operators--including black-box optimizers, mutations, and crossovers--to facilitate exploration and induce diversity. Scaling these methods becomes increasingly costly as policies and behavior spaces grow larger. Building on the Soft QD view of quality-diversity optimization, we introduce \textbf{Re}pulsive \textbf{P}opulation \textbf{P}olicy \textbf{O}ptimization (\textbf{RePPO}), which adapts PPO to jointly optimize a compact population of diverse, high-quality policies using a population-dependent, dynamically shaped reward. On standard continuous-control tasks, RePPO achieves a strong quality-diversity tradeoff compared with state-of-the-art QD-RL methods. Ablations further characterize this tradeoff by examining how each component affects individual quality and collective diversity. To assess RePPO's scalability, we extend our evaluation to learning from pixels and fine-tuning large language models. In pixel-based tasks, where large policies make most traditional QD-RL methods infeasible, RePPO maintains quality on par with standard PPO while achieving substantially higher diversity. Its successful application to language model fine-tuning further demonstrates, for the first time, that QD-RL methods can scale to this domain. Together, these results show that directly optimizing compact populations with RePPO provides a scalable approach to QD-RL across increasingly complex domains.
  </details>

- **[When Can a Learned Design Surrogate Be Trusted in a Self-Driving Microfluidics Laboratory? An Offline Closed-Loop Framework Built and Tested on Six Public Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36395)**  `arXiv:2610.36395`  
  _Zhiyuan Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-driving laboratories couple a learned design surrogate to an instrument, and on every new target domain it must answer four questions: when it can be trusted, how many target measurements it needs, which to make first, and when to stop adapting, switch models or escalate. We state a framework making each measurable (a competence ratio, a budget $k^*(\varepsilon)$, a time-to-target and a priced decision) and instantiate it without wet experiments on six public droplet-microfluidic and liposome tables as offline laboratories: 107 leave-one-group tiers whose rows are hidden labels. A sealed, pre-registered programme (two sealed negative validations and a pre-registered test on three more tables) and seven frozen closed-loop protocols, each run twice, yield 23 findings. Shift cost is stratified by shift type; no label-free statistic certifies individual candidates; random-split model-family rankings predicted neither the sealed nor the loop rankings. Warm-start residual boosting returns a surrogate to $1.25\times$ its development reference within 20 target rows in 94% of chemistry tiers (27% under a strict noise-floor reading), 90% of laboratory and 40% of device tiers, and a budget planned from the shift type on other tables is not measurably worse on a new one. In the loop, one gain survives drift and shuffled-label controls on all tiers, carried by the sparsely sampled ones: proposing at once and warm-starting on every measurement reaches a 10% target in 2.80 measurements against 3.31 for the reference, and in 5.72 against 11.35 for random proposals on 301 hard units at a 3% tolerance. No label-free acquisition rule survives the shuffled-label control, no interval-aware proposal or other model family does better, and no threshold rule on the tested diagnostics beats always continuing once escalation is priced. No wet-lab validation is claimed.
  </details>

- **[SpecLST: Spectral-Enhanced Long-Short Term Spatio-Temporal Multimodal Modeling for Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36393)**  `arXiv:2610.36393`  
  _Kenan Kang, Matthew M. Y. Kuo, Weihua Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modeling multi-scale dynamics in urban road networks is essential for intelligent transportation systems but is often hindered by the computational cost of long-range historical context and the challenge of fusing heterogeneous modalities. Existing spatio-temporal frameworks face a trade-off between sequence length and memory overhead, while frequently neglecting the transient effects of exogenous factors such as road incidents and weather. To address these limitations, we propose SpecLST, a frequency-enhanced long-short-term spatio-temporal multimodal framework. SpecLST leverages a fast Fourier transform-based frequency-domain representation module to efficiently encode multi-week historical dependencies, bypassing the memory bottlenecks of standard attention mechanisms over raw long sequences. Heterogeneous context is integrated through attention-based fusion, followed by a sparse Mixture-of-Experts backbone with graph augmentation for spatio-temporal representation learning. Additionally, a frequency-enhanced residual head isolates high-frequency transients, improving sensitivity to abrupt traffic anomalies. At the 60-minute horizon, SpecLST achieves mean relative error reductions of 14.10% in MAE, 10.35% in RMSE, and 15.78% in MAPE across eight neural forecasting baselines and four datasets, with the dataset- and metric-specific average reductions ranging from 5.63% to 22.21%.
  </details>

- **[Reliability-Aware Short-Term Glycemic Risk Prediction Using an Anchor-Based Comparative Framework of Sequence, Multimodal, and State-Updating Digital-Twin Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36363)**  `arXiv:2610.36363`  
  _Pir Bakhsh Khokhar, Carmine Gravino, Fabio Palomba_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anticipating short-term hypoglycemic and hyperglycemic risk is essential for proactive diabetes management. However, existing machine learning approaches often use inconsistent evaluation protocols and emphasize predictive performance over reliability. This study introduces an anchor-based benchmarking framework integrating longitudinal glucose sequences and multimodal patient information. Using the T1DiabetesGranada dataset, 5,503,441 standardized prediction anchors were constructed from 736 individuals with type 1 diabetes to evaluate hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia prediction at 30-, 60-, and 120-minute horizons. Three neural architectures, a Hybrid LSTM-Transformer, a Multimodal Digital Twin, and a State-Updating Digital Twin with gap-aware longitudinal state progression, were compared under identical patient-level validation protocols. Beyond predictive performance, the evaluation examined calibration, classification thresholds, explanation reliability, cross-model disagreement, and robustness to controlled perturbations. No statistically significant predictive differences were observed after Holm correction, although the models exhibited complementary reliability characteristics. The Multimodal Digital Twin achieved the lowest mean Brier score, while the State-Updating Digital Twin demonstrated the lowest mean expected calibration error (ECE-10). The Hybrid LSTM-Transformer showed strong explanation faithfulness and stability under its temporal attribution protocol. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating digital-twin models beyond predictive accuracy and emphasize the need for reliability assessment and external validation before clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[Freeze, Don't Clone: Fine-Tuning with Expressive Policies Without Retaining Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36247)**  `arXiv:2610.36247`  
  _Sami Nur Islam, Johan Obando-Ceron, Doina Precup, Aaron Courville, Pablo Samuel Castro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline-to-online reinforcement learning uses offline pretraining to accelerate subsequent online learning. While many methods assume the offline dataset remains available throughout fine-tuning, there are many settings where this is not the case. Prior work attributes failures in this setting primarily to critic recalibration under distribution shift. We identify a complementary actor-side failure: once the offline data disappears, continued behavioral cloning (BC) no longer preserves the offline behavior distribution and instead refits the behavioral model to the agent's limited, and nonstationary, online experience. We demonstrate that stopping online behavioral fitting while preserving the pretrained expressive prior substantially improves no-retention fine-tuning across implicit flow Q-learning (IFQL), flow Q-learning (FQL), and action-chunked RL (QC) on seven OGBench domains. We trace this failure to a behavioral feedback loop: continuing online behavioral cloning rapidly erases the pretrained prior, inducing representational collapse and action diversity loss that starves the critic of useful exploration. Freezing the prior breaks this cycle, preserving offline coverage while allowing value learning to drive continuous improvement.
  </details>

- **[Concorde: Geometry-Aware Link Prediction via Decoupled Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36204)**  `arXiv:2610.36204`  
  _Ege Demirci, Ambuj Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Link prediction remains difficult in sparse graphs, where diffusion heuristics lack connectivity and geometric methods suffer spectral instability when coupling learned representations with diffusion operators. We introduce Concorde, an energy-based framework that resolves this by decomposing link likelihood into three structurally decoupled branches: (i) a parameter-free spectral anchor that establishes permutation-invariant topological stability; (ii) a mixed-curvature residual on a learnable $\mathbb{H}^{d_h}_{c_h} \times \mathbb{S}^{d_s}_{c_s}$ product manifold, regularized by a feature-aware Ollivier-Ricci curvature proxy; and (iii) a continuous vector field on the tangent bundle of hyperbolic space for non-local structural alignment across topological gaps. The branches are fused via learned gating and trained with an N-pair ranking loss aligned with retrieval-style evaluation. Across five benchmarks spanning sparse, dense, featureless, and attributed regimes, Concorde establishes state-of-the-art early retrieval precision, nearly doubling P@10% over leading baselines in sparse settings.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Evidence Accumulation for Graph-Based Learning in Dynamic Data-Driven Applications Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36190)**  `arXiv:2610.36190`  
  _Moises Ponce, Sarah Harkins Dayton, Ioannis Schizas, Vasileios Maroulas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are widely used in modern machine learning, yet most existing frameworks remain fundamentally static, limiting their applicability in Dynamic Data Driven Application Systems (DDDAS) where observations arrive sequentially under uncertainty. We introduce B-EAGLe (Bayesian Evidence Accumulation for Graph-Based Learning), a probabilistic framework inspired by CBGT-Net (cortico-basal ganglia-thalamic network) that reformulates graph classification as a sequential evidence accumulation process. B-EAGLe combines a GNN encoder with a Bayesian accumulator that incrementally updates posterior beliefs over class labels as new observations arrive, committing to a decision only when sufficient confidence is achieved. Experiments on CIFAR10 demonstrate competitive performance together with principled confidence estimation under sequential observations.
  </details>

- **[SpatialPC: Deep Computation with Sublinear Local Credit Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36139)**  `arXiv:2610.36139`  
  _Benjamin Prada, Shion Matsumoto, Ankur Arjun Mali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On a chain of $N$ states, any local learning rule with bounded communication range needs $\Theta(N)$ rounds before the loss can influence the earliest parameters, and this latency is usually attributed to depth. We show that it is a property of the communication geometry rather than of depth. For $N=m^d$ states placed at constant density in $d$ dimensions, a light-cone argument gives an $\Omega(N^{1/d})$ lower bound on the number of bounded-range rounds that any local algorithm needs before its gradient estimates can be sign-correct at every parameter. We construct a unit-range, degree-$2d$ directed acyclic graph that contains a Hamiltonian computation of length $N-1$ and in which every state lies within directed distance $d(m-1)=\Theta(N^{1/d})$ of the output, matching the lower bound. In the realizable-circuit model, depth and credit depth are therefore independent parameters: represented depth $\Theta(N)$ and causal credit latency $\Theta(N^{1/d})$ coexist, with a separation of order $N^{1-1/d}$. SpatialPC realizes this separation within predictive coding. Credit is propagated by adjoint prediction dynamics (APD), a local update whose $K$-round state on a settled acyclic graph is the truncated reverse-path sum $a^K=\sum_{k<K}(J^\top)^k b$, with error exactly $(J^\top)^K a^\star$. Graph geometry fixes when credit can first arrive at a state; the Jacobian-weighted path tail fixes how many further rounds accurate credit requires. A sparse two-dimensional Moore compiler retains the $N-1$ backbone with $\tfrac32N-2$ unit-range edges and has measured worst-node reachability $\sqrt N+2$ up to $N=65{,}536$, where a 65,535-edge backbone is reached in 258 local hops. With $T=2\sqrt N$ rounds, SpatialPC keeps finite-round task gradients aligned with settled gradients (cosine $0.93$ on MNIST and $0.97$ on Fashion-MNIST at $N=256$) and reaches $97.6\%$ and $88.1\%$ test accuracy after 20 epochs.
  </details>

- **[Instruction Capacity: Behavior and Degradation of Language Models under Controlled Load](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36095)**  `arXiv:2610.36095`  
  _Hennadii Kutomanov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quality with which language models follow instructions falls as the load grows, yet there is still no quantity that expresses the limit of a model in units of load with an uncertainty. We propose an instrument in which the load consists of rules that replace single-token words in a text without meaning: such an instruction is atomic, the rules are independent by the construction of the task, and the outcome of every application of a rule is determined by a computable reference answer. Every occurrence of a word to be replaced becomes a Bernoulli trial with a known position in the answer and a known rule number. The statistical model first measures the shape of the dependence of the probability of a right replacement on the text length, the word position, the replacement density, the number of rules and the rule number, then describes it by smooth laws and restores a reference level that does not depend on the choice of the coding. The model yields the instruction capacity - the number of rules held at a given share of right replacements - with an uncertainty. A measurement of GPT-4o mini (2958 requests, 642,430 trials) gave regular laws for every load factor and for the collapse of the answer; the instruction capacity was about 37-38 rules at a share of right replacements of 0.95, and about 18-19 rules at the full replacement density. The code of the instrument, the measurement data and the passport are open, and every number of the passport is recomputed from the published points. The passport of a model serves as the calibration of a future meter of the instruction load of prompts.
  </details>

- **[DuplexTune: Coupled-State Replay for Memory-Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36009)**  `arXiv:2610.36009`  
  _Paul Raj, Simon Caldwell, Daniel Whitmore, Mutlu Mete_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse expert execution does not remove the stored states needed to adapt a mixture-of-experts language model. We present DuplexTune, a framework for replaying coupled hidden states through pretrained attention and expert modules. Trainable width bridges connect two residual streams to the backbone. Bridge warm-up precedes backbone adaptation, with router weights kept frozen. We analyze the implicit state-recovery equation and identify the convergence and replay conditions needed for reconstruction. On Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B, the single-H800 evaluation reports 38.8 GB peak memory, versus 64.8 GB for supervised fine-tuning with checkpointing, and throughput of 25.1 versus 19.9 samples/s. DuplexTune achieves 67.2% MMLU, 75.6% GSM8K, and 7.69 MT-Bench, with a weaker multilingual score than the checkpointed baseline. The results characterize a configuration-dependent tradeoff among state storage, throughput, and adaptation quality.
  </details>

- **[Layerwise Repacking: Variable-Length Hybrid-Attention Training without an Extra Redistribution Pass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35989)**  `arXiv:2610.35989`  
  _Jiahao Zhang, Junwei Feng, Dongliang Huang, Xiaoling Zhou, Yiqiao Liao, Chengru Song_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Variable-length training creates conflicting parallelism requirements across the layers of a hybrid-attention model. The longest samples require wide sequence-parallel (SP) groups of 16 or 32 ranks for full attention, whose cost grows quadratically with sequence length. Sparse-attention layers, dominated by near-linear MoE work, can instead balance most evaluated workloads within node-local SP8 groups. Reusing the wide groups for these layers pushes their all-to-all communication across nodes, where the observed effective rate is 7.0x lower. Yet switching to smaller groups changes which ranks own each sample; a separate redistribution at every full-sparse boundary would introduce another data transfer.Layerwise Repacking keeps each SP16/32 parent for full attention and repacks its samples into node-local sparse children at SP{1, 2, 4, 8}, where hidden states persist between full layers. The layout change rides on the two existing full-layer Ulysses sites: at each, one variable-size all-to-all performs both the head exchange and the sample repacking, so no redistribution pass is added. Because the balance the children can reach depends on which samples share a parent, an auxiliary parent solver rebalances parent membership before the children are formed.On all 22 steps with SP16/32 parents among the first 200 training steps of a 1F3S hybrid-attention model (one full-attention layer per three sparse-attention layers), repacking alone reduces the mean end-to-end time by 5.95% and raises model FLOPs utilization (MFU) by 3.75 percentage points over the baseline with repacking disabled; with the improved solver, end-to-end time falls by 7.61% and MFU rises by 4.83 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[Let the Gate Decide: Per-Lookup Precision for Quantized Conditional Memory in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35985)**  `arXiv:2610.35985`  
  _Tianqi Chen, Xunchao Li, Linchuan Xie, Guanghua Yu, Jianchen Zhu, Peisong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conditional memory layers, such as DeepSeek's Engram, attach large hashed n-gram tables to a language model, and removing them from DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash costs 36.8 PopQA points. Low-bit quantization loses long-tail facts first, and the standard remedy, giving more bits to frequently accessed rows, backfires: refining lookups by access frequency is worse than refining them at random. We trace this to the gate that scales each retrieved row. A lookup's quantization error is weighted by its gate, which is small for frequent n-grams, large for the rare n-grams of long-tail entities, and computable from a 1-bit code. GateRQ therefore stores each row as a ladder of 1-bit residual codes, keeps level 1 in GPU memory to compute the gate, and reads deeper levels only for the lookups the gate ranks highest. Without retraining, GateRQ cuts GPU table memory by 7.8x on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, which raises vLLM throughput by 11%-31% over FP8 tables, while losing about 1 PopQA point at 1.50 bits read per lookup and 0.2 points at 2.10 bits across two models.
  </details>

- **[Multiple Leak Localization in Water Distribution Networks through Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35972)**  `arXiv:2610.35972`  
  _Arvin Ajoodani, Sara Nazif, Pouria Ramazi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Leak localization in water distribution networks is challenging when multiple leaks occur simultaneously, particularly under sparse monitoring. Existing data-driven methods often assume a single or predefined number of leaks or require extensive enumeration of leak combinations. This study proposes a graph neural network (GNN) model that localizes a variable number of simultaneous leaks without specifying the leak count in advance or exhaustively generating every possible combination of leak locations for training. The model is evaluated on two benchmark networks of different sizes, the 31-node Hanoi network and the larger 92-node NET3 network, under variable demand, sparse pressure-sensor coverage, variable leak magnitudes, and varying numbers of simultaneous leaks. The GNN outperforms multilayer perceptron, support vector machine, K-nearest neighbors, and random forest classifiers by up to 12% and 20% in accuracy respectively in Hanoi and NET3 networks. Consistent performance across both networks supports the scalability of the proposed approach.
  </details>

- **[Generalization in Learning: Eyring Formula and Predator-prey Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35965)**  `arXiv:2610.35965`  
  _S.V.Kozyrev, I.A.Lopatin, Alexander Pechen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, generalization in machine learning is investigated using a predator-prey model. We overview biological and chemical interpretation of the stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics and generative adversarial network. For a particular example of recovering an unknown function by a polynomial of fixed degree from a set of noisy data, the predator-prey model provides better approximation compared to the gradient descent method. Further, thermodynamical arguments, in particular Eyring formula, are used to explain grokking (delayed generalization) phenomenon in machine learning.
  </details>

- **[Condition-Enhanced Generative Adversarial Network for Aerodynamic Configuration Design of Commercial Aircraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35963)**  `arXiv:2610.35963`  
  _Youzheng Yu, Jiaqing Kou, Weiwei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative machine learning provides an end-to-end framework for aerodynamic design optimization by generating diverse aerodynamic configurations conditioned on target performance. However, the generated designs may violate geometric constraints or deviate from the prescribed aerodynamic performance. Therefore, we propose a Condition-Enhanced Generative Adversarial Network (CEGAN) that embeds geometric and aerodynamic information into the generative process. A condition-informed loss function is developed by combining a geometry-guided feasibility penalty with an aerodynamic performance penalty evaluated by surrogate models trained on the same dataset. Additionally, an output filtering mechanism discards infeasible designs using these surrogate models, ensuring compliance with prescribed conditions. The method is demonstrated on aircraft configurations similar to the Cessna Citation X. Each configuration is represented by 28 shape parameters, whose aerodynamic performance is evaluated using OpenVSP. Interpolation and extrapolation tests are conducted for different network architectures and conditioning strategies. Compared with the baseline model, CEGAN reduces the average generation error by at least 36% for interpolated conditions and by at least 46% under extreme extrapolation conditions, while successfully producing diverse and physically feasible designs that extend beyond the training distribution. Therefore, the proposed CEGAN framework offers a robust and accurate tool for rapid aerodynamic configuration exploration during the conceptual design phase.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge distillation across teacher architectures for compact diabetic retinopathy grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35961)**  `arXiv:2610.35961`  
  _Mossa Manisa, Habiba Jahan Priya, A.K.M. Fakhrul Hossain_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diabetic retinopathy grading from retinal fundus photographs is important but difficult to deploy on resource-constrained hardware. We conducted a controlled study of offline response-based knowledge distillation for five-class diabetic retinopathy grading on APTOS 2019, comparing ResNet-50, MobileNetV3-Large, and Swin Transformer-Tiny (Swin-T) teachers distilled into a MobileNetV3-Small student. A 3 x 4 x 2 grid of teacher, temperature, and hard-label weight was screened at one seed, while six configurations and the no-distillation baseline were evaluated across three seeds. The highest observed single-seed quadratic weighted kappa was 0.9093 (macro-F1 0.6851) for Swin-T at temperature 4 and hard-label weight 0.7; the corresponding three-seed mean was 0.9024, compared with 0.8804 for the baseline. The student used 1.52 M parameters and 5.94 MB, representing an 18.07x parameter reduction relative to Swin-T. Temperature and hard-label weight effects were teacher- and configuration-dependent, while small performance differences were sensitive to random seed. Knowledge distillation produced a compact student with competitive performance under the evaluated protocol, but independent external validation remains necessary.
  </details>

- **[From Slices to Volumes: Comparing 2D, 2.5D, and 3D Residual Networks for Pulmonary Nodule Malignancy-Risk Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35960)**  `arXiv:2610.35960`  
  _Bahodir Nazarov, Zineb Gharib, Fatima Zazoul_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: To compare 2D, 2.5D, and 3D residual networks for pulmonary nodule malignancy-risk classification and evaluate momentum-contrast pretraining, mild augmentation, and exclusion of indeterminate ratings.Methods: A retrospectively prepared LIDC-IDRI cohort contained 2,654 nodules labeled benign, indeterminate, or malignant using median radiologist ratings. Patient-disjoint splits comprised 1,876 training, 382 validation, and 396 test nodules. Representations included one axial slice, five neighboring slices, and a local CT volume. Each track evaluated a randomly initialized baseline, momentum-contrast pretraining followed by fine-tuning, and supervised training with mild augmentation. Checkpoints were selected by validation Macro-F1. The 2D experiments used three seeds; 2.5D and 3D results were single-run estimates. A separate binary experiment excluded indeterminate nodules.Results: Augmented models achieved test Macro-F1 values of $0.6134\pm0.0075$ for 2D, $0.6537$ for 2.5D, and $0.5403$ for 3D; the 2D uncertainty denotes population standard deviation across seeds. Corresponding accuracies were $0.6128$, $0.6869$, and $0.5455$. Augmentation improved Macro-F1 within every track, whereas the tested pretraining recipes reduced it. Binary Macro-F1 values were $0.8227$, $0.8664$, and $0.7554$, respectively.Conclusion: Augmented 2.5D achieved the highest observed performance. Differences in preprocessing, optimization, capacity, and replication limit attribution to dimensionality. Labels reflect radiologist-assessed malignancy risk rather than pathology-confirmed cancer. Code and experimental results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ResCom: Activation Residual Compensation for Large Language Model Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35955)**  `arXiv:2610.35955`  
  _Ning Yang, Bohan Cui, Zhuowen Liu, Yuting Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit weight--activation quantization reduces the memory and computation needed to deploy large language models, but activation rounding can still degrade accuracy after calibration. Reconstruction from rounded inputs cannot distinguish activations within the same quantization bin. We propose ResCom, a low-rank correction that uses the activation rounding residual to compensate for the resulting layer-output error. An output-aware calibration procedure selects residual directions by their contribution to output reconstruction and computes the correction in closed form, without gradient training or updates to the calibrated weights. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weights and activations, rank-32 attention corrections reduce WikiText-2 perplexity from 7.708 to 7.147, improve MMLU by 2.89 percentage points, and raise five-shot GSM8K accuracy from 23.96 to 36.16 percent. Evaluations on models from 1B to 70B parameters and on held-out C4 test the method beyond this setting. The evaluated FP32 implementation adds 2.25 percent to 4K prefill time; a separate compact-storage implementation retains a 27.7 percent peak-memory reduction relative to FP16.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum-Driven Deep Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Far-Range Rocket Landing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35954)**  `arXiv:2610.35954`  
  _Atin Chowdhury, Piyush Joshi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous vertical landing of reusable launch vehicles is a critical yet formidable challenge in aerospace engineering, primarily due to highly nonlinear dynamics, stringent precision require- ments, and inherent environmental uncertainties. While reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising alternative to traditional model-based control, existing approaches frequently rely on flat training paradigms and highly simplified initial conditions, drastically limiting their real-world applicability. This paper proposes a comprehensive curriculum-driven deep reinforcement learning framework designed to master both far-range navigation and precision landing. By employing a three-stage curriculum augmented with a critic warm-up procedure and a rehearsal mechanism to prevent catastrophic forgetting, alongside a high-dimensional observation vector utilizing a full 3 X 3 rotation-matrix, the agent is trained to navigate from an initial horizontal distance of 1,000 to 1,500 units vastly farther than prior benchmarks before executing a soft touchdown. Crucially, we intro- duce a redesigned reward function that reduces unnecessary computational overhead and addresses limitations of earlier formulations, including poorly calibrated reward components and potentially conflicting optimization objectives. The redesigned function bounds the per-step reward magnitude by (O(1)), while its progress term accumulates the total distance closed over the trajectory. The available 100,000-episode results report a 99.98% success rate for the proposed policy and 68.31% for the baseline under the evaluation conditions. In the evaluation, the proposed policy retained 69.1% of its initial fuel capacity and achieved a mean final distance from the pad centre of 7.07 units, compared with 13.10 units for the baseline. Landing visualizations are available in Video 1 and Video 2.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Research Arena: Benchmarking and Advancing Auto-Research in Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35866)**  `arXiv:2610.35866`  
  _Xi Chen, Chongjian Ge, Yiran Xu, Yiqun Mei, Henrik I. Christensen, Hao Tan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As autonomous research systems become more capable, benchmarks must both evaluate their performance and support meaningful research progress. We introduce Auto-Research Arena, a multi-target benchmark for evaluating autonomous research systems and discovering ideas in language modeling. Arena supports diverse research objectives within a shared experimental environment by separating admissibility from improvement: model quality and resource constraints determine whether a result qualifies, while the selected target measures progress among admissible results. We instantiate Arena in language model pretraining with nine targets spanning model, data, training, and inference efficiency, and evaluate seven research harnesses using two foundation models. Arena clearly differentiates existing harnesses across research objectives, with several sophisticated methods outperformed by our simple sequential search baseline, indicating substantial headroom in current auto-research systems. Beyond evaluation, Arena reveals a substantially broader frontier of model and system ideas aligned with active research directions. Reusing these discoveries in a new quality-driven search yields lower training loss under matched data and FLOPs and lower held-out BPB at the same model size. Together, Arena provides a common framework for benchmarking autonomous research and expanding its scope in language modeling. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Multi-Fidelity Training Reshapes Uncertainty Decomposition in Atomistic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35560)**  `arXiv:2610.35560`  
  _Matteo Tolloso, Davide Bacciu, Luigi Bonati_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-fidelity training, which combines abundant low-fidelity data with scarce high-fidelity labels, is increasingly common in atomistic modeling, but its effect on predictive uncertainty remains largely unexplored. We investigate this question using deep ensembles of mean-variance MACE models trained on ANI-1x, contrasting high-fidelity training from scratch with low-fidelity pretraining followed by fine-tuning. We characterize aleatoric, epistemic, and total uncertainty throughout training and under two distribution shifts: unseen molecular systems and higher-energy configurations. We find that pretraining reduces prediction error and improves uncertainty-based ranking across all test sets, but does not yield well-calibrated predictive variances; calibration deteriorates more severely under energy extrapolation. Training-dynamics analysis localizes most of this calibration degradation to high-fidelity optimization, during which the learned aleatoric variance becomes overconfident on held-out data. Moreover, multi-fidelity fine-tuning produces a more effective uncertainty-based acquisition function than high-fidelity training from scratch. These findings show that uncertainty ranking and calibration capture distinct properties and must be assessed separately in multi-fidelity atomistic models, especially under extrapolation.
  </details>

- **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Streaming Vision--Language Inference on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309)**  `arXiv:2610.35309`  
  _Yilong Li, Chengpo Yan, Yijing Zeng, Hanlei Wu, Mingjun Song, Suman Banerjee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge SoCs pack CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, yet vision--language model (VLM) runtimes keep generation on one engine and leave the others idle. Streaming VLMs expose further parallelism: new frames arrive while earlier queries are still being answered. Engines differ in compute and memory access and contend when they co-run, so no single placement of stages wins across SoCs and workloads. Backend-local KV state keeps other engines from continuing the work. \name coordinates heterogeneous accelerators to execute stateful VLM inference concurrently. It separates a stream's versioned context from each engine's KV state, so any engine can continue the stream by reusing, importing, or reconstructing that state. Stateful Streaming Generation ingests new frames on one engine while another answers, and Concurrent Sequence Generation serves different ready requests in parallel. State-aware dispatch sends each request to the engine expected to finish it first. Each answer reads a fixed, published version of its history and matches its serial reference. \name beats the strongest single-engine baselines on Apple M4 Max, Rockchip RK3588, and Qualcomm QCS6490, even when they reuse context. On two RK3588 video streams, it delivers $19.6\%$ more throughput than the best single engine with context reuse and $27.2\%$ more than parallel engines without shared state. Cross-engine KV import cuts first-token latency by a factor of $4.6$ over prefix recomputation. Stateful Streaming Generation delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of serial execution, and state-aware dispatch cuts query completion time by up to $72.7\%$.
  </details>

- **[STeP-TS: Shared Temporal Filters with Pruning for Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35128)**  `arXiv:2610.35128`  
  _Hanne Raum, Jasper Hoffmann, Joschka Boedecker_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series forecasting requires capturing temporal dependencies across multiple scales while accounting for both shared and variable-specific dynamics. Compact forecasting models have emerged as an attractive alternative to increasingly large architectures, but preserving strong predictive performance within tight computational and memory budgets remains challenging. We propose a compact one-layer convolutional framework that learns a shared bank of multi-scale temporal filters of each variable. Lightweight variable-specific scalar weights determine the contribution of each temporal component, enabling the model to capture shared temporal structure while adapting to variable-specific dynamics. The learned weights and filter-output energy further provide a natural signal for structured pruning of convolutional filters, reducing computational complexity with minimal degradation in forecasting performance. We additionally introduce an amplitude-adaptive normalization mechanism that preserves variable-dependent temporal amplitudes. Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate forecasting performance competitive with state-of-the-art methods while requiring substantially fewer parameters and inference FLOPs and achieving low per-sample latency.
  </details>

- **[SEEK: Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge against Frontier Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34365)**  `arXiv:2610.34365`  
  _Cheng Gui, Xiang Zheng, Yutao Wu, Hanxun Huang, Yige Li, Kaidi Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Science agents increasingly package domain expertise as reusable skills that plan analyses, select tools, and execute workflows. This modularity creates a confidentiality risk: the interactions that deliver a skill's capability can also reveal the skill itself. We build an extensible benchmark of 40 protected skills across eight open- and closed-source science-agent platforms, comparing direct, fixed-strategy, and bandit-guided iterative probing under reference-based and reference-free evaluation. Leakage is pervasive: all eight platforms (100%) leak at least one protected skill, and four reach 100% attack success under a single direct request. To stress-test platforms that resist simple probing, we propose SEEK (Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge), a black-box, self-evolving red-teaming agent that consolidates response-derived evidence in persistent memory and evolves reusable attack skills, using only user-visible responses. On 12 skills from SpatialAgent and Biomni, SEEK achieves 100% attack success while reducing the mean first-success round by 31.8% and 38.2% relative to bandit-guided probing. Against a confidentiality policy that blocks all three benchmark protocols, SEEK still extracts one of six protected skills. These findings show that current science agents do not reliably protect their skills and that defenses must be evaluated against adaptive, multi-turn attackers.
  </details>

- **[INTRA: Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34056)**  `arXiv:2610.34056`  
  _Yuxuan Xia, Wenbo Lu, Shaoyi Zheng, Shengjie Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers achieve strong performance across modalities but are bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of attention. Sparse attention can reduce this cost, but effective sparsity must preserve useful long-range communication and map well to GPU hardware. We introduce INTRA--Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention--a hardware-aware static sparse attention design based on a relay-communication view under Computational Query Set (CQS) constraints. Rather than directly covering all token-to-token interactions within each sparse layer, INTRA uses complementary sparse groups to provide short mediated communication paths across layers. Scatter forms relay groups that sample broadly across local neighborhoods, while Gather reconnects these groups in subsequent layers. Under a partition model, this structure guides CQS-compatible sparse patterns with global reachability. Our CQS-aware FlashAttention-style kernel computes structured indices at runtime, loads selected non-contiguous tokens, and packs them densely for computation. On an A100, INTRA achieves a $1.53\times$ speedup in $2K$ FLUX.1-dev generation, with better measured image quality than evaluated CLEAR configurations after LoRA self-distillation. On LLaMA-3.1-8B, it improves long-context prefill efficiency with strong teacher-forced retrieval performance under matched LoRA adaptation. Attention-only benchmarks further show up to $8.4\times$ and $10.4\times$ speedups over MoBA and density-constrained NSA, respectively. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Climate downscaling with likelihood functional flow matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33482)**  `arXiv:2610.33482`  
  _Zhi-Song Liu, Helmi Toropainen, Ammar Kheder, Michael Boy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decisions about heat, frost and flooding are made locally, yet reanalyses and climate models resolve tens of kilometres. Generative downscalers add kilometre-scale detail with uncertainty, but are tied to a grid, costly to sample and lack a tractable likelihood. Here we introduce CD-FFM, a functional flow-matching model that treats the high-resolution field as a continuous random field that one model can query at any location and resolution. Its velocity acts pointwise, so spatial variability comes from a noise prior fitted to the observed spectrum and every observation has an exact likelihood. Downscaling ERA5 to 1 km daily temperature over Fennoscandia, CD-FFM lowers the continuous ranked probability score by 27-31% against regression and 12-18% against a forty-times larger neural operator in each of three test years, and outperforms a CorrDiff-style diffusion model while sampling about fifty times faster. The same model transfers to precipitation and makes large kilometre-scale ensembles affordable.
  </details>

- **[The Cost of an Unknown Horizon for the Last Iterate of Clipped SGD under Heavy-Tailed Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33381)**  `arXiv:2610.33381`  
  _Yuxing Peng, Zhiqing Tang, Weijia Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training is often stopped at a data-dependent time, so guarantees for the last iterate must hold at all times simultaneously. We determine the optimal time-uniform rate for the last iterate of clipped SGD on smooth convex objectives under heavy-tailed noise with a bounded central $p$th moment, $1<p\le2$. A schedule without horizon or confidence inputs attains $\tilde O(t^{-\beta_p})$ at all times, with $\beta_p=2(p-1)/(3p-1)$, also almost surely, and no rule that steps along the stochastic gradient at its current iterate attains $\tilde O(t^{-r})$ for any $r>\beta_p$, even with adaptive step sizes and thresholds. This rate lies between the optimal rate $O(T^{-\alpha_p})$ for a known horizon $T$, $\alpha_p=(p-1)/p$, and the previous time-uniform rate $\tilde O(t^{-2(p-1)/(3p)})$ (Chezhegov et al., ICLR 2026); under finite variance, the exponents are $1/2$, $2/5$, and $1/3$. Their conjecture that a better schedule reaches the optimal rate thus holds with a known horizon (Liu; Jiang et al.) but fails without one. Querying gradients at the running average attains $\tilde O(t^{-\alpha_p})$ at all times, whereas heavy-ball momentum with geometric forgetting and polynomially bounded steps cannot improve on $\beta_p$.
  </details>

- **[Tree Depth and Sample-Quality-Associated Features: Two Hidden Factors in Boruta Selection for RNA-Seq Data Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33307)**  `arXiv:2610.33307`  
  _Tareque Mohmud Chowdhury, Abu Raihan Mostofa Kamal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Boruta is a widely used method for selecting gene panels from RNA sequencing data, and its most used implementation recommends Random Forest trees of depth 3 to 7, an untested setting. We tested whether this setting changes which genes are selected, using pan-cancer data from The Cancer Genome Atlas (10,469 tumors, 33 cancer types) and breast cancer PAM50 subtyping (981 tumors, 5 subtypes). A leakage-free protocol fitted every label-dependent step inside training folds, measured every effect against seed-to-seed variation, and held out a locked test set per task. In pan-cancer data, shallow trees changed the selection far more than a new seed did: the top-500 overlap with unlimited-depth selections was 0.37 at depth 5, against 0.84 to 0.93 between seeds at depth 5 or unlimited depth. Shallow trees confirmed 3.5 times fewer genes, missed markers of large classes and produced less accurate 50-gene panels (balanced accuracy 0.809 vs 0.889). The effect vanished once the cap reached about 60 to 75% of the depth that unrestricted trees grow to, set mainly by the number of classes, not sample size, and leaving depth uncapped cost no accuracy in either task. The effect replicated in a second cross-validation repeat and under permutation importance. About a third of pre-filtered candidates also tracked sample quality within class; a within-class filter removed them without loss of test accuracy (balanced accuracy 0.943 vs 0.942 with 1,000 genes). We recommend uncapped trees, or a cap chosen from the realized tree depth, and a within-class quality filter before Boruta.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Multi-View Fusion for Urban Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31850)**  `arXiv:2610.31850`  
  _Sean Bin Yang, Zongyi Xu, Jilin Hu, Ying Sun, Kristian Torp, Bin Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban region representation learning encodes heterogeneous urban signals, such as mobility flows, points of interest, and land-use patterns, into unified embeddings for downstream tasks, including check-in prediction, crime forecasting, and service demand estimation. Existing approaches rely on globally shared fusion and joint optimization, implicitly assuming uniform modality importance and naturally balanced multi-objective training. However, urban data is inherently heterogeneous. Our empirical analysis reveals two structural issues: (i) modality utility varies markedly across regions, causing static fusion to overemphasize signals from certain modalities; and (ii) heterogeneous objectives exhibit persistent gradient imbalance, causing representational bias and unstable training. We present PRISM, a self-adjusting framework for adaptive multi-view learning. PRISM adapts sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing to learn region-specific view allocations, while a Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID)-inspired regulation mechanism mitigates gradient conflicts during optimization. Experiments on three large-scale urban datasets show that PRISM achieves more competitive performance than strong multi-view baselines, while providing more stable optimization behavior and more balanced region representations.
  </details>

- **[A Fix for Softmax and Cross-Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31642)**  `arXiv:2610.31642`  
  _Jonathan T. Barron, Dan Gnanapragasam, Michael Tschannen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard implementations of softmax, log-softmax, and cross-entropy have two numerical bugs. The first bug, Softmax Collapse, rounds the target logit's gradient to zero if that one logit is much larger than the others, resulting in gradients that no longer sum to zero. The second bug, Softmax Cancellation, occurs when student logits are close to teacher logits, and produces non-zero gradients even when the student and teacher logits are equal. We present simple implementations of log-softmax, softmax, and cross-entropy that fix both bugs. Our fix eliminates the previously observed "slingshot" phenomenon, speeds up grokking with weight decay, prevents accuracy collapse without weight decay, stops student networks from drifting away from the teacher during distillation, and shows that after memorization, logit growth is bounded by an equilibrium between weight decay and Adam's $\epsilon$ hyperparameter.
  </details>

- **[Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31544)**  `arXiv:2610.31544`  
  _Zeropy Surio, Tobias Engel, Mei-Lin Chou, Anika Raman, Samuel Okafor_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Counterfactual branching estimates this credit by re-running the episode after an alternative decision, but the branch draws its sampling noise independently of the factual episode, so the outcome difference mixes the effect of the decision with the luck of everything that follows. We observe that in agentic search the retriever, the corpus, and the grader are deterministic, so an episode is a deterministic function of the policy's decisions and its sampling noise. Twin worlds are therefore realizable: the same noise, a different decision. We introduce TWICE (TWIn-world Credit Estimation), which keys the Gumbel noise of every token by turn and line, rolls out counterfactual twins that inherit the factual episode's downstream noise, and turns their paired outcome differences into turn-level advantages for policy-gradient updates on actor-sampled trajectories; only the actor is deployed. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-\rho$, where $\rho$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $\Delta$ falls from $\Theta(\Delta^{-2})$ to $\Theta(\Delta^{-1})$. A square-root rule spends the twin budget where outcomes are predicted to disagree per unit cost. On seven question-answering benchmarks, TWICE improves the average exact match of GRPO by 5.3 and 4.5 points on Qwen2.5-3B and 7B, outperforms tree-search and counterfactual-branching baselines, and uses 68% fewer auxiliary tokens than VinePPO.
  </details>

- **[Loopholing All the Way: Language Modeling with pure Self-Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30068)**  `arXiv:2610.30068`  
  _Adrien Deschenaux, Nadezhda Ilieva, Justin Deschenaux, Manuel Madeira, Dorina Thanou, Aurélien Lucchi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) use Self-Conditioning (SC), or Loopholing, to improve their performance. While SC is traditionally seen as an auxiliary heuristic, its empirical success raises the question: can a continuous generative model be based purely on Self-Conditioning? We answer affirmatively with LAW (``Loopholing All the Way''), which propagates the Expected Embedding under the denoiser's prediction across steps, decoding the tokens once, at the end. On Sudoku, LAW outperforms previous Discrete and Continuous Diffusion Models, including Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. On Maze, LAW is the most accurate from 2 to 64 NFE. After training on TinyGSM and sampling at temperature 1 with 1024 NFE, LAW solves 42.2% of GSM8K problems against 34.5% for uniform Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. Additionally, fine-tuning a pre-trained Uniform Diffusion Model (Duo) with LAW increases its accuracy from 17.2% to 48.1%. Finally, on OpenWebText, LAW pushes the Pareto frontier and approaches Autoregressive Models (ARMs) with 64 NFE. Find our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27895)**  `arXiv:2610.27895`  
  _Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.
  </details>

- **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920)**  `arXiv:2610.25920`  
  _Haoyu Li, Zeren Shui, Yuhao Zhang, Ferhat Erata, Benjamin Mikek, Murali Krishna Ramanathan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof goals into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.
  </details>

- **[First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24816)**  `arXiv:2610.24816`  
  _Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.
  </details>

- **[Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21375)**  `arXiv:2610.21375`  
  _Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.
  </details>

- **[MemWM: Associative Memory-Enhanced Latent World Models for Long-Horizon Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19683)**  `arXiv:2610.19683`  
  _Yuwei Sun, Siyu Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models trained via latent dynamics offer a scalable path for embodied planning, yet existing methods suffer from representational collapse, computational overhead, and limited long-horizon capability. We introduce MemWM, a memory-enhanced latent world model that augments JEPA-style prediction with a content-addressable memory bank. MemWM learns high-level representations directly from observations and uses them to condition predictions via adaptive layer normalization, complementing low-level action-conditioned dynamics within a shared latent space. To prevent memory slots from collapsing into identical representations, we propose two complementary losses that encourage structural diversity across slots and balanced utilization across time steps. On PushT, TwoRoom, Reacher, and OGBCube, MemWM attains an average success rate of 89.9%, matching DINO-WM (89.3%) while using 4$\times$ fewer trainable parameters and no pretrained encoder, and improving over LeWM by 4.1%.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13020)**  `arXiv:2610.13020`  
  _Zhicheng Cai, Hanlin Wu, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability.
  </details>

- **[Learning Pareto Stationary Fronts via Single-Pass Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06397)**  `arXiv:2610.06397`  
  _Elina Rojin Celik, Marcos Medeiros Raimundo, Isabel Valera_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose MOSEL (Multi-Objective Stackelberg Efficient Learning), a framework for a posteriori multi-objective optimization (MOO) in deep neural networks that recovers a full front of Pareto stationary solutions at the computational cost of standard single-objective training. MOSEL reformulates the problem as a bilevel optimization problem that leverages network modularity to decouple representation learning from objective-preference alignment. Casting the bilevel optimization problem as a Stackelberg game enables solving the original a posteriori MOO problem in a single forward-backward pass. As a result, MOSEL matches the time and memory efficiency of standard single-objective training while enabling scalable Pareto stationary front learning. Empirically, MOSEL uncovers diverse and optimal Pareto frontiers in strongly conflicting settings (e.g., fairness-accuracy). Remarkably, even in weakly conflicting regimes such as multi-task learning, it consistently converges to solutions closer to the utopia point, outperforming both standard single-objective training and specialized multi-task learning methods. These results highlight the broader potential of a posteriori MOO learning as a pathway to efficiently learn more diverse and robust representations, ultimately improving generalization.
  </details>

- **[Strategic Multi-Agent Learning for Interpretable Action Valuation of All Players in Football](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05961)**  `arXiv:2610.05961`  
  _Kenjiro Ide, Taiga Someya, Kohei Kawaguchi, Keisuke Fujii_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Valuing player actions in football, a complex multi-agent system, requires accounting for strategic interactions among 22 players, including off-ball movements and defensive positioning. Existing reinforcement-learning-based valuation methods commonly incorporate other players through the observed state while retaining independent-agent value formulations. To our knowledge, we introduce the first Markov perfect equilibrium (MPE)-inspired framework for valuing the actions of all 22 players using real football tracking and event data, formulating each possession as a finite-horizon multi-agent dynamic game. To improve interpretability, the framework uses Expandable Decision-Making States (EDMS) and decomposes each Q-value into a successor-feature basis and a linear reward-weight vector. The value basis is estimated through a two-stage procedure combining linear temporal-difference initialization with nonlinear iterative refinement from observed state-action transitions. Using tracking and event data from 95 J1 League matches, we compare the proposed formulation with an independent reinforcement learning baseline under both Position and Velocity States (PVS) and EDMS representations. With EDMS held fixed, the independent baseline assigns the highest value to forward movement in 99.21% of evaluated off-ball states, whereas the most frequent direction under the proposed formulation accounts for 17.63%. Qualitative analyses illustrate context-dependent off-ball valuations and highlight limitations of the current state representation for defensive positioning. Overall, the framework provides an MPE-inspired approach to interpretable action valuation in football, enabling analysis under alternative tactical reward weights.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable Synthetic Medical Tabular Data Generation for Clinical Decision Support Using Fuzzy Cognitive Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00391)**  `arXiv:2610.00391`  
  _Michael Vasilakakis, Dimitris K. Iakovidis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
